=== PDF PAGE 3 ===
INTRODUCTION 
Welcome to Merton Council’s Budget book for 2026-2030, which brings 
together financial information in the form of the council’s budget and the Medium 
Term Financial Strategy over the next four years. Although it is a four-year plan, 
it will be refreshed every year to ensure we are on target and to take account of 
changing needs and priorities. 
 
As the work of the council encompasses such a wide and diverse range of 
services, we have tried to include all the necessary information needed to 
understand what we are doing and why we are doing it. This information should 
give you a detailed picture of how the council will be budgeted over the next 
four years. To help understand some of the more complex areas of the 
council’s business a Glossary of Terms has been included. 
The Medium Term Financial Strategy incorporates details of the money the council 
has coming in (revenue) and the money and assets it has in place (capital) and 
how it will spend and invest this money over the four year planning period, through 
the Capital and Treasury Management Strategies. 
 
Other key resources in place to enable the council to manage the Budget Book 
include how we identify and manage the risks the council may face in delivering 
services, through the Risk Management Strategy. 
 
The book also contains proposed Savings & Growth for 2026-2030 and the 
Revenue Estimates and Fees & Charges for 2026-27. 
We appreciate that there is a huge level of detail in the book, but hope it helps 
you to understand what the council is trying to achieve and how we intend to 
succeed. If you have any comments or questions on the content of this plan, 
then please contact: zoe.church@merton.gov.uk

MEDIUM TERM FINANCIAL STRATEGY 2026-2030 
 
SECTION 1: GENERAL FUND REVENUE BUDGET 2026/27, COUNCIL TAX 
STRATEGY AND MEDIUM TERM FINANCIAL STRATEGY 2026-30 
 
1. Background to Financial Planning 
1.1 The approach to Medium -Term Financial Planning provides a great deal of 
flexibility in decision making and enables the impact of alternative options to be 
modelled. This has assisted with progress towards a balanced budget over the 
four-year plan. 
1.2 The MTFS has been re-priced and rolled forward a year so that each year from 
2026/27 to 2029/30 starts from the approved budget 2025/26 and is built up 
showing the cumulative effect of variations over the period. Council on 26 
February 2025 agreed the Budget 2025/26 and MTFS 2025 -29. Whilst a 
balanced budget was set for 2025/26 there was a gap remaining in future years 
which needs to be addressed, as shown in the following table:- 
 
 
Table 1: Budget Gap Council 26 February 2025 
(Cumulative)

£000 
2029/30 
£000 
MTFS Gap (Council February 2025) 5,142 6,500 8,667 13,886 
 
1.3 Reports to Cabinet on 22 September 2025, 20 October 2025, 19 January 
2026 and 16 February 2026 presented updated information to Members, 
setting out the financial implications as assumptions were reviewed and 
decisions made. 
1.4 The decisions made in respect of each element of the MTFS impact on the 
overall financial position of the Authority. To make progress towards 
balancing the budget over 2026-30, an overall package of options and 
variables that need to be included in the budget were modelled, enabling the 
impact of different scenarios to be seen. 
1.5 The main issues in formulating the budget for 2026/27 and the development 
of the MTFS 2026-30 continued to be the ongoing impact of high inflation 
and consequential impact on pay and prices, the increasing cost of adults 
and children’s social care, the cost of SEND and schools, and the increasing 
levels of homelessness and associated costs. These issues will continue to 
have implications across the MTFS planning period and as far as possible 
the financial impact has been recognised in the budget reports to Cabinet. 
1.6 In addition, the government has introduced as part of the Local Government 
Finance Settlement for 2026/27 to 2028/29, changes made in respect of the 
Fair Funding Review 2.0. This includes a business rates revaluation and 
resetting of the Business Rates Retention Scheme. The financial impact of 
these decisions has been included in the Medium Term Financial Strategy 
2026-30. 

=== PDF PAGE 5 ===

1.7 Setting a Balanced Budget 
1.7.1 Resource and expenditure projections were continually updated throughout 
the year to inform Members and officers of the overall position for 2026-30. A 
four-year forward plan is presented, but it is still a legal requirement that a 
balanced (i.e. fully funded) budget is achieved for 2026/27. There are a 
number of ways in which a balanced budget is achieved: 
 
• Reducing expenditure/costs; 
• Identifying new savings or additional income; 
• Adjusting the level of Council Tax ; 
• Using unallocated reserves as a temporary measure pending the 
implementation of the options above. 
 
1.7.2 Budget savings proposals were formulated within the overall constraints of the 
resource projections. The proposals were approved in principle by the Cabinet 
and then considered by Overview and Scrutiny Panels as part of the scrutiny 
process. Meetings of the Overview and Scrutiny Panels and the Committee 
took place during November 2025 and January/February 2026 and their 
feedback on the Council’s spending proposals was considered by Cabinet on 
16 February 2026. Due to the remaining financial gap a third tranche of 
savings was considered by Cabinet on 16 February 2026. 
1.7.3 The Medium-Term Financial Strategy (MTFS) for the Council supports the 
achievement of the budget and any financial implications arising from the 
agreed plan are included in financial planning for 2026/27 and beyond. The 
MTFS brings together all of the key elements relating to the Council’s financial 
position which are reflected in the General Fund, and the Capital Programme. 
1.7.4 The MTFS which was reported to Cabinet on 19 January 2026 showed that 
the budget gap was:-

1.7.5 As with previous years, the Medium-Term Financial Strategy is a multi-year 
approach and this includes a review of total planned expenditure and income 
over the period 2026-30. 
1.7.6 There were further changes to some of the key budget variables as more 
information has become available and the details are set out in this report. 
1.7.7 One of the key objectives is to balance the budget and there has been a major 
improvement in recent years in the ability to look over a long-term period and 
identify significant savings over more than one year. 
1.7.8 The Medium-Term Financial Strategy framework aims to enable strategic 
resource allocation and investment decisions to be made that allow for 
services to transform and change at different times in the future and which

would otherwise be very difficult to plan under an annual planning framework. 
This improved long-term financial planning process is the best way to ensure 
that budgets are robust and sustainable.

2.2.1 Outlook for inflation 
CPI inflation had fallen since January 2026 to 3.2%. Although above the 2% 
target, it is forecast to fall back towards target more quickly in the near term. 
Reflecting restrictive monetary policy, and consistent with evidence of 
subdued economic growth and building slack in the labour market, pay growth 
and services price inflation also continued to ease. 
The Consumer Prices Index (CPI) rose by 3.2% in the 12 months to 
November 2025, down from 3.6% in the 12 months to October. On a monthly 
basis, CPI fell by 0.2% in November 2025, compared with a rise of 0.1% in 
November 2024. 
The Consumer Prices Index including owner occupiers' housing costs (CPIH) 
rose by 3.5% in the 12 months to November 2025, down from 3.8% in the 12 
months to October. On a monthly basis, CPIH fell by 0.1% in November 2025, 
compared with a rise of 0.2% in November 2024. 
Food and non-alcoholic beverages, and alcohol and tobacco made the largest 
downward contributions to the monthly change in both CPIH and CPI annual 
rates. 
Core CPI (CPI excluding energy, food, alcohol and tobacco) rose by 3.2% in 
the 12 months to November 2025, down from 3.4% in the 12 months to 
October; the CPI goods annual rate slowed from 2.6% to 2.1%, while the CPI 
services annual rate eased slightly from 4.5% to 4.4%. 
Core CPIH (CPIH excluding energy, food, alcohol and tobacco) rose by 3.5% 
in the 12 months to November 2025, down from 3.7% in the 12 months to

October; the CPIH goods annual rate slowed from 2.6% to 2.1%, while the 
CPIH services annual rate eased slightly from 4.6% to 4.5%. 
The RPI rate for November 2025 was 3.8%, which is down from 4.3% in 
October 2025.

2.3 Extended Producer Responsibility for Packaging 
In 2025/26 the government (Defra) introduced funding for Extended Producer 
Responsibility (EPR) for packaging. The MTFS 2025-29 included £2.951m 
grant for 2025/26 but to be prudent £1m was included for subsequent years. 
On 4 November 2026 the council was notified that £3.252m is the anticipated 
total EPR for packaging payment for Financial Year 2026 to 2027. 
There is therefore additional funding of £2.252m expected for 2026/27 and 
this assumption was included in the MTFS 2026-30. 

=== PDF PAGE 8 ===
5  
2.4 Concessionary Fares 
London Councils administers the Freedom Pass scheme on behalf of the 32 
boroughs and the City of London Corporation. Each year, negotiations take 
place between London Councils (on behalf of boroughs) and the transport 
operators. These include TfL, the Rail Delivery Group (RDG) for national rail 
services (excluding the London Overground and Elizabeth Line network which 
is managed by TfL) and local bus service providers for the cost of the 
Freedom Pass usage on non-TfL bus routes. 
The past 12-months have seen demand continue to recover from the lows of 
the coronavirus pandemic. Freedom pass journeys are up on last year, but 
some of the effects of the pandemic can still be seen in the settlement figures, 
which remain marginally lower than pre-pandemic. As a result, next year’s 
settlement will cost c. 11.81% more than this year’s. 
The methodology for the apportionment of cost between boroughs was 
established following an arbitration award in 2008. It established the principle 
of apportioning costs according to usage and using a two-year rolling average 
of the previous two years’ journeys to establish the next year’s cost. In order 
to apportion costs between boroughs, London Councils has obtained usage 
data from Oyster clicks on the various transport modes; bus, underground, 
DLR, tram, London Overground and National Rail. 
London Councils identified areas of the settlement methodology for further 
review and updated and refined its approach to cost modelling – providing 
more regular and longer-term forecasts of costs to assist with financial 
planning at borough level. 
London Councils issued a revised 5-year forecast for the Freedom Pass levy, 
updated for latest inflation and economic projections and assumptions on the 
journey demands beyond 2026.

2.5 Collection Fund 
2.5.1 The Collection Fund is a statutory fund separate from the General Fund. It 
accounts for income from Council Tax and from NNDR (Business Rates). 
 
2.5.2 The Collection Fund accounts are kept on an accrual basis and a surplus or 
deficit for the year in the collection fund is estimated accordingly. The draft 
accounts for 2024/25 include the following surplus/deficit for Council Tax and 
NNDR as at 31 March 2025:-

2.5.3 A review of the Collection Fund, related bad debt provisions, write offs, 
appeals and collection rates in 2025/26 and anticipated collection rates in 
2025/26 was undertaken. With respect to Business Rates, the NNDR1 return 
was submitted which analyses the estimated Business Rates for 2026/27, and 
estimated surplus/deficit as at 31st March 2026 estimated Section 31 Grant 
and the allocations between Merton, Central Government, and the GLA. For 
2025/26 and 2026/27 Business Rate Retention, the shares will be 33% central 
government, 37% GLA and 30% Merton.

3. Budget Monitoring 2025/26 and Ongoing Budget Pressures 
3.1 Based on expenditure as at the third quarter (Period 9, December 2025), 
there is a forecast net adverse variance for 2025/26 of £0.785m, consisting of 
unfavourable variances of £5.925m in services offset by a favourable variance 
of £5.139m in corporate provisions. There may be issues identified from 
monthly monitoring which need to be addressed in setting the budget for 
2026-30. 
3.2 The main pressures are in the following services:- 
• Children’s Social Care (CSC) and Youth Inclusion £3.909m 
• Housing – Temporary Accommodation/Homelessness £7.154m 
3.3 The CSC position is driven primarily by pressures from placements (CP) and 
packages for children with disabilities (FSS). 
3.4 The total number of placements in temporary accommodation at the beginning 
of April 2025 was 700 compared to 539 one year earlier and based on P9 
monitoring is forecast to remain constant at 750 by the end of March 2026. 
3.5 Additional Pressures and growth with Ongoing Impact  
As previously mentioned, service directorate’s budgets have been under 
pressure during the current financial year, 2025/26, Work was undertaken to 
mitigate pressures and to identify those pressures that require ongoing 
support needed in order to meet service demands.

4.1 Local Government Finance Settlement 2026-30 and Fair Funding 
4.1.1 The Local Government Finance Settlement is a multi-year Settlement for 
2026/27 to 2028/29. 
 
4.1.2 The Final Local Government Finance Settlement was published on 9 
February 2026.

4.1.6 The Local Government Finance Settlement 2026-29 sets out the 
government’s proposals to deliver the Fair Funding Review 2.0 reforms 
through locally retained business rates and grant funding allocations, over the 
next 3 years. This includes plans for the total Fair Funding Allocation for each 
council in 2026-27, comprised of Revenue Support Grant (RSG), Local 
Authority Better Care Grant (LABCG), and Baseline Funding Levels (BFLs), 
as well as wider changes to the business rates retention system and funding 
consolidation. 

=== PDF PAGE 13 ===
10  
4.1.7 The changes arising from the Settlement, driven by the Fair Funding Review 
represent a fundamental change to local government funding and have 
resulted in a major redistribution of resources. Due to a transition arrangement 
spread over three years, the full effect of the changes arising from the Fair 
Funding Review 2.0 will not be fully realised until 2028-29. 
4.1.8 It is important to recognise that whilst Core Spending Power increases year 
on year for Merton between 2026/27 to 2028/29 this is based on the 
assumption that council tax is increases by 5% each year and that the council 
tax base increases by c. 1.6%. 
4.1.9 The changes to the funding regime being introduced from 2026/27 are 
complex. The settlement is based on a major review of all local authorities’ 
needs and resources and has resulted in significant changes to government 
resource allocations and the assumption that councils increase their council 
tax by the maximum permitted under Referendum Principles 
4.1.10 The changes arising from the Fair Funding Review 2.0 are estimated to have 
the following negative impact on council funding: -

4.1.12 Under Fair Funding a higher Needs Allocation and a low Resource Adjustment 
will result in a higher funding allocation. 
Merton’s Needs share is ranked as low and is 28th lowest out of 32 London 
boroughs and 16th out of 19 Outer London boroughs. 
Also, if a council’s actual average Band D council tax had been used rather 
than the national average, Merton’s Resource Adjustment would be c. £118m. 
 
4.2 Council Tax Referendum Threshold 
The Final Settlement confirmed that the referendum threshold for principal 
local authorities such as Merton is as follows:- 
• For 2026-27, the relevant basic amount of council tax is excessive if the 
authority’s relevant basic amount of council tax for 2026-27 is 5% 
(comprising 2% for expenditure on adult social care, and 3% for other 
expenditure), or more than 5%, greater than its relevant basic amount 
of council tax for 2025-26 
 
4.3 SEND White Paper and DSG Safety Valve and Deficit Calculation 
In the Final Local Government Finance Settlement 2026-29 the government 
announced that they are introducing support for local authorities’  Dedicated 
Schools Grant (DSG) deficits in phases. The first phase will tackle historic 

=== PDF PAGE 15 ===
12  
deficits accrued up to the end of 2025-26. All local authorities with SEND 
deficits will be eligible in 2026-27 to receive a grant covering 90% of their High 
Needs-related DSG deficit accrued up to the end of 2025-26, subject to each 
local authority submitting and securing the Department for Education’s 
approval of a local SEND reform plan. 
The government will confirm the detail on further support for deficits arising in 
2026/27 and 2027/28 before the accounting override set out in Regulation 30L 
of The Local Authorities (Capital Finance and Accounting) (England) 
Regulations 2003 (“the Statutory Override”) ends from 1 April 2028. From 
2028-29, SEND spending will be covered by the overall government 
Departmental Expenditure Limit (DEL) budget, meaning local authorities are 
not expected to fund future SEND costs from general funds, once the 
Statutory Override ends at the end of 2027-28. 
 
First Phase of Support 
The government will make available a High Needs Stability Grant covering 
90% of historic deficits accrued up to the end of 2025-26 to all local authorities 
with SEND deficits. 
This grant will be paid in Autumn 2026, subject to each local authority 
submitting and securing the Department for Education’s approval of a local 
SEND reform plan. 
The government say that “resolving DSG deficits is a shared priority for 
central and local government, working alongside schools, health partners, 
early years settings and post-16 providers. Therefore, local authorities are 
expected to set out a clear pathway towards an inclusive system in line with 
the wider vision for SEND reform. The Department for Education will 
commission local area partnerships to develop these plans.” 
As part of this, the government will share best practice from previous 
programmes, including Safety Valve and Delivering Better Value, to support 
local authorities to develop local plans. Additionally, each local area will be 
provided with advisers to help ensure that spending is effective, efficient, and 
focused on improving outcomes for children and young people 
Statutory Override 
The Statutory Override will remain in place until the end of 2027-28. While it 
remains in effect, all DSG deficits including any proportion of the historic 
deficit up to 2025-26 not covered by grant (“the residual deficit”) will remain in 
the associated statutory reserve (“the unusable reserve”) and will not affect 
local authorities’ wider financial positions. The Statutory Override will end on 
31 March 2028. Therefore, local authorities will need to plan to be able to 
meet the cost of the residual deficit from their own resources in 2028-29, 
including setting aside appropriate reserves in the preceding years. 
Future Phases of Support 
The government acknowledge that SEND reform will take time to fully embed 
and local authorities will need further support. For deficits that arise in 2026-
27 and 2027-28, local authorities can expect that the government will continue 

=== PDF PAGE 16 ===
13  
to take a similarly appropriate and proportionate approach to such support, 
though it will not be unlimited. 
 
4.4 Government Approach to Simplifying the local government funding landscape 
The government aims to simplify the funding landscape, and give authorities 
greater certainty and flexibility. 
As part of the Fair Funding Review 2.0 the government set out its ambitions 
for 2026-27 aiming to deliver the biggest programme of revenue funding 
simplification to date. It created four large consolidated grants that bring 
together funding for similar service areas, as well as rolling funding into 
Revenue Support Grant wherever possible. The government also set out its 
proposed principles for the scope of revenue funding simplification. 
To be rolled into Revenue Support Grant the government requires that a grant 
should generally be paid to all local authorities or, depending on the service, 
to all upper tier or all lower tier authorities. The government has simplified an 
unprecedented 36 revenue funding streams worth over £56 billion over the 
three years (2026-27 to 2028-29) of the multiyear Settlement. This will be 
delivered by: 
 
1. Creating four new consolidated grants from 2026-27, 
• The Homelessness, Rough Sleeping and Domestic Abuse Grant that 
consolidates four funding streams; 
• The Children, Families and Youth Grant that consolidates five funding 
streams, including the 2025-26 Children and Families Grant; 
• The Public Health Grant that brings together four funding streams with the 
existing Public Health Grant; and 
 
2. The Crisis and Resilience Fund that consolidates two funding streams. 
• rolling 18 funding streams into the 2026-27 Revenue Support Grant 
• bringing all prior business rates under indexation compensation funding, 
into the 2026-27 Revenue Support Grant. 
• Bringing together funding for Mayoral Strategic Authorities into the 
Mayoral Capacity Fund 
 
The government say that the package of funding simplification will give local 
authorities more certainty of their income than they have had previously. 
Authorities will receive fewer grants, each of a larger value, and have more 
flexibility around how they are spent. This will reduce administrative burdens, 
and support the delivery of key outcomes that meet local needs. 
 
Whilst simplification and greater flexibility is to be welcomed, it will create 
some short term issues for the council in ensuring that funding is allocated to 
the correct budget areas. 

=== PDF PAGE 17 ===
14  
5. Capital Financing and Treasury Management 
5.1 Capital financing costs are derived from the draft capital programme which is 
included in the Capital Strategy and estimated revenue funding is built into the 
MTFS for the level of borrowing that is expected. 
5.2 Details relating to how the capital programme has changed from that 
approved by Council in March 2025 to the capital programme for 2026-30 are 
discussed in the Capital Strategy and related appendices. The capital 
programme continued to be subject to major review as part of monthly 
monitoring and the process of developing the Capital Strategy and 
Programme for 2026-30. 
 
5.3 In addition to reviewing the capital programme, focus was also maintained on 
the treasury management aspects of funding the programme, to improve 
forecasts of available funding and to minimise as far as possible the costs of 
funding capital. This included improved cash flow forecasting to enable the 
programme to be funded from capital receipts and internal resources for as 
long as possible to take advantage of current low levels of interest. Work also 
continued to ensure that grants and contributions are utilised effectively, and 
this work will continue into the closing of accounts process for 2025/26 to 
ensure that the revenue impact of the capital programme is minimised in 
2026/27 and beyond. 
 
5.4 Revenue Implications of Current Capital Programme 
It is important to ensure that the revenue and capital budgets are integrated 
and not considered in isolation. The revenue implications of capital 
expenditure can quickly grow if the capital programme is not contained within 
the Council’s capacity to fund it over the longer term. 
5.5 There have been limited additions to the capital programme due to the current 
financial challenges and Budget Managers have been asked to further review 
current schemes in the programme to either reduce, defer or delete them. Any 
resulting revisions to the programme will be reported to Cabinet on an 
ongoing basis, along with any changes required. The current capital provision 
and associated revenue implications of the approved capital programme, 
based on November 2025 monitoring information and maximum use of capital 
receipts were reported to Cabinet in January 2026.

7.7 Financial Resilience – CIPFA Index 
On 19 January 2026, CIPFA announced the release of the latest version of the 
Resilience Index (2024/25), 
The CIPFA Financial Resilience Index shows an English council's position on 
a range of measures associated with financial risk highlighting where 
additional scrutiny may be required. The selection of indicators has been 
informed by extensive financial resilience work undertaken by CIPFA, public 
consultation and stakeholder engagement. 
CIPFA advise that “Section 151 officers may also reference the index in their 
annual report to the council on the proposed budget and medium-term 
financial strategy, particularly in their assessment of the robustness of the 
budget and the adequacy of reserves.” 
The resilience index uses data from the Revenue Expenditure and Financing 
England Outturn Report ("RO Forms") submitted by Local Authorities to the 
Ministry of Housing, Communities & Local Government (MHCLG). 
While recognising some limitations in the returns (e.g. reflecting the previous 
financial year) they provide a consistent and transparent starting place for a 
discussion on financial resilience that can be tracked over time. 
The chart below summarises Merton’s financial resilience against a number of 
key financial measures and indicates that overall Merton’s financial risk based 
on 2024/25 outturn is good. 

=== PDF PAGE 22 ===
19  
 
 
The index provides a platform to facilitate constructive dialogue between 
those charged with decision-making and governance. It uses historic 
comparative data to support medium term financial planning. 
This model offers a transparent source of data to support Section 151 officers 
in their duty to employ good financial management. 
Whilst based on 2024/25 outturn data Merton’s financial resilience appears 
robust, this has to be seen in the context of a changing financial environment 
with rising demand for services such as temporary accommodation due to 
increasing levels of homelessness, and social care, both adults and children’s. 
In addition, there will added pressure from the outcome of the Fair Funding 
Review which indicates an overall reduction in grant by c. £13m over the next 
three years. 
 
8. Business Rates Funding 2026/27 
8.1 In the 2026-27 financial year, the government is introducing a business rates 
revaluation and a new structure with five different multipliers in England. This 
replaces the previous system which used temporary Retail, Hospitality, and 
Leisure (RHL) relief. 
8.2 The government is also undertaking a Business Rates Retention System 
Reset. As part of the Fair Funding Review 2.0 and Local Government Finance 
Settlement 2026-27 the government is fully redistributing growth in business 
rates around England. Accrued above-baseline growth in business rates 
revenues, which councils have been able to retain since 2013–14, will be 
redistributed based on updated assessments of needs. Following this 
redistribution of past growth, councils will be able to benefit from future growth 
in business rates revenues from 2026–27 onwards, until the system is reset 
again. 
 
8.3 In summary, the Reset is a technical exercise to update the government’s 
assessment of how much business rates are available (updating Business 
Rates Baselines); and where they are needed (updating Baseline Funding 
Levels). 


=== PDF PAGE 23 ===
20  
8.4 This redistribution of local authority income across local government is 
managed via ‘top-ups’ and ‘tariffs’. Authorities whose income exceeds their 
assessed need will pay a tariff. Authorities whose income is less than their 
assessed level of need will receive a top up payment. 
 
8.5 The following table demonstrates the fundamental role that Business Rates 
income has in funding Merton’s services:-

The government will continue to operate a safety net threshold as part of 
Business Rates Retention. The government is increasing the level of the 
safety net to protect an authority’s business rates income for 2026-27 to 100% 
of their set Baseline Funding Level (BFL). This will provide increased certainty 
for local authorities when budgeting for business rates income in 2026 
amongst the backdrop of major business rates reforms, including the reset 
and tax policy changes. 
 
As proposed in the Fair Funding Review 2.0, the increased protection 
provided by the safety net will gradually scale back to the current 92.5% level 
over the course of the multi-year Settlement period. The government 
proposes that for 2027-28, the safety net will be set at 97% of local 
authorities’ BFLs, before returning to its standard level of 92.5% in 2028-29. 
 
9. Council Tax Bases, GLA Precept and Levies 
9.1 The Localism Act 2011 amended the legislation regarding the calculation 
of council tax. It also provides for a council tax referendum to be held if an 
authority increases its relevant basis amount of council tax in excess of 
principles determined by the Secretary of State. 
 
9.2 Council Tax Referendum Principles 
 
The Government proposes the following package of referendum principles for 
2026-27 for authorities like Merton: 
 
• a core council tax referendum principle of up to 3% for shire counties, 
unitary authorities, London boroughs, the GLA general precept, and fire 
authorities 
• an Adult Social Care (ASC) precept of 2% on top of the core principle for 
local authorities with responsibility for adult social care. 
 
9.3 For Merton, a Council Tax increase of 5% (3% core + 2% Adult Social Care) 
would be regarded as excessive but, for example, a core principle of 2.999% 
plus Adult Social Care precept of 2% (i.e. 4.999%) would be in accordance 
with the thresholds set.

9.5 Greater London Authority Precept and Other Levies 
The Greater London Authority (GLA) sets a budget for itself and each of the 
four functional bodies: Transport for London, the London Development 
Agency, the Metropolitan Police Authority, and the London Fire and 
Emergency Planning Authority. These budgets together form the consolidated 
budget. 
The proposed Band D precept for council taxpayers in the 32 London 
boroughs is £510.51 – a £20.13 or 4.1% increase compared to 2025/26. This 
increase comprises £15 for MOPAC (Met Police) and £5.13 for the London 
Fire Brigade. This is in line with the maximum increase permitted under the 
GLA’s referendum limit – which is a monetary one – as set out in the 
provisional local government finance settlement. The proposed precept for 
council taxpayers in the City of London - which has its own police force - is 
£176.38 (an increase of £5.13 - 3% - which excludes the £15 relating to the 
Met Police).

10. Council Tax and Fair Funding Review 2.0 
10.1 The collection rate is an important element of council tax funding and features 
in the government’s Fair Funding Review 2.0 as well as the government’s 
consultation paper “Modernising and improving the administration of council 
tax” which included changes to collection and enforcement practices. 
10.2 In the Fair Funding Review 2.0 the government state that they “will use the 
Council Tax base of a local authority as a measure of this Council Tax raising 
ability. The strength of Council Tax bases is usually down to several factors, 
including historical house prices and land values, and, in general, is not an 
outcome of the decisions that local authorities make.“ 
10.3 The government’s Fair Funding Review sets out how the government 
determines local authority funding allocations, bringing together an updated 
assessment of relative need, a resource adjustment, and transitional 
arrangements. To equalise for the Council Tax base of a local authority, the 
government set an assumed or ‘notional’ level of Council Tax for all authorities 
in the resource adjustment. 
 
10.4 The assumption that the government make for the notional Council Tax level 
determines the amount of funding that is distributed according to the updated 
needs assessment, and the size of the relative resource adjustment for 
individual local authorities. The government say that “the notional Council Tax 
level therefore effectively sets the level of equalisation in the funding system, 
with a higher notional level of Council Tax meaning greater equalisation of the 
Council Tax base. Greater equalisation of the taxbase prevents 
disadvantaging those areas with weaker taxbases.” 
 
10.5 The government has set a notional Council Tax level that it believes will 
achieve the objective of full equalisation. To fully equalise against the Council 
Tax base, the government has set the notional Council Tax level at the 
average Band D level of Council Tax in England for authorities in scope of 
these reforms. This means that for 2026-27 the notional council tax level used 
is £2,063, for 2027- 28 it is £2,164, and for 2028-29 it is £2,269. Merton’s 
average Band D council tax in 2025/26 is £1,604.05. 
 
10.6 This does not mean that all councils will set their Council Tax at this level, but 
that the government calculate their central grant funding and locally retained 
business rates in such a way to ensure that all authorities can provide the 
same level of services to their residents, irrespective of their ability to raise 
Council Tax. 

=== PDF PAGE 27 ===
24  
10.7 In terms of collection rate, in its calculations , the government will be 
assuming an 100% council tax collection rate within the resource adjustment. 
Although no local authorities achieve 100% collection, it is the government’s 
view that this is important to retain a clear incentive for all authorities to 
reduce avoidance of council tax. A uniform collection rate assumption 
supports the reform principle of simplicity, and ensures that authorities always 
benefit from increasing their collection rate. 
10.8 For 2026-27, Merton’s estimated council Tax Base assumes a collection rate 
of 98.75% (unchanged from 2025/26) 
10.9 Council Tax Assumptions 
10.9.1 Council Tax setting is a key element of a council’s funding strategy. Most 
council’s are constrained by the level of council tax increase that they can set 
by Referendum Principles. 
 
10.9.2 Local authorities are required to determine whether the amount of council tax 
they plan to raise is ‘excessive’. The Secretary of State sets thresholds of 
excessiveness, known as ‘referendum principles’, for different classes of 
authority. Since 2016-17, these thresholds have also included a ‘social care 
precept’, providing higher thresholds for authorities with social care 
responsibilities. The thresholds are finalised alongside the annual Local 
Government Finance Settlement each February. Any authority proposing an 
excessive increase in council tax must hold a local referendum and obtain a 
‘yes’ vote before implementing the increase. An authority proposing an 
excessive increase must also make substitute calculations, based on a non-
excessive council tax level. This is implemented if the excessive increase is 
rejected in the referendum. 
 
10.9.3 For 2026-27 the government have announced that they will maintain core 
referendum principles as they were in 2025-26 over the multi-year Settlement. 
For London boroughs this is 3% for core services and 2% for Adult Social 
Care Precept. 
In the Local Government Finance Settlement 2026-29 the government has 
assumed that councils increase their council tax by the maximum allowed. 
Council Tax is a key component of local government funding and has an 
impact on the level of funding that councils receive. Clearly the council has to 
carefully consider its options when setting its council tax level. 
The MTFS 2026-30 agreed by Council in February 2026 includes the following 
assumptions for a council tax increase:- 
 
Table 32: Council Tax assumptions in 
MTFS (Council February 2026 ) 
 
2026/27 
 
2027/28 
 
2028/2G 
 
202G/30 
Council Tax - Core Services 0% 2% 2% 2%

=== PDF PAGE 28 ===
25  
The funding from the ASC precept is passported to the service in full so any 
adjustment to the amount agreed has a nil impact on the MTFS budget gap 
but will impact on the ASC budget. 
Council Tax Strategy Proposed by Council 
Having identified further savings contained in February 2026 from its review of 
service departments and corporate budgets and taking into account the 
continuing cost of living pressures experienced by residents, Council agreed 
to freeze the level of general Council Tax with an increase of 2% in the Adult 
Social Care precept to help fund care for older and vulnerable residents within 
Merton. 
Freezing the level of general Council Tax produces a permanent real terms 
reduction in Council Tax yield of up to £3.8m. This is a permanent and 
compounding loss of funding for the Council budget. This has been mitigated 
through the savings agreed as part of the 2026-27 budget process. 
The decision to not increase general council tax by the referendum limit may 
also have implications for the council’s future “funding floor” going forward. 
10.9.4 Funding Floor 
In the government’s technical consultation on funding for 2026-29 it states 
that it intends “to ensure that local authorities see their income protected by a 
specified amount across the multi-year Settlement. The rationale for this 
policy would be to prevent funding reductions that could pose a financial 
sustainability risk in these local authorities. However, such a policy involves a 
direct trade-off with allocating funding where the government consider it to be 
needed most according to the updated Settlement Funding Assessment.” 
It further states that the government “would also propose continuing the 
existing policy that any protection offered through a funding floor assumes 
local authorities use their full council tax flexibility.” It is therefore assumed that 
if the council should in future fall below the funding floor level, whether 
additional support is provided towards this will depend on whether the council 
has increased its council tax by the maximum set out in the referendum 
principles between now and any such event. 
 
13.9.5 To summarise, as part of its Fair Funding Review 2.0 the government has 
made a number of assumptions around Relative Need Formulae (RNF) and 
Council Tax which have fundamentally changed the assessment of needs and 
resources. Merton’s RNF rating is low compared to other London boroughs. 
The assumptions on resources included in the calculation of resources are 
based on an average council tax of £2,063 compared to Merton’s £1,604.05 
and that 100% of council tax is collected. Therefore Merton’s resources 
element is higher than it can achieve based on it’s current Band council tax. 
The combination of a low needs rating and a relatively high resources rating 
leads to a reduction in grant of c £13.4m over the Spending Review period 
These are discussed in more detail in Appendix 2.

11. CONCLUSIONS 
 
11.1 It is a statutory requirement that the council sets a balanced budget in 
2026/27. In accordance with sections 52ZY and 68 of the Local Government 
Finance Act 1992 (’the 1992 Act’), section 139A of the Local Government 
Finance Act 1988, and section 230 of the Local Government Act 1972 
authorities are required to supply information required to measure whether 
any proposed Council Tax increase is in excess of the principles laid down 
requiring a local referendum. 
 
11.2 The Government return is the Council Tax Requirement form CTR1 and 
authorities are required to complete and submit this to the Ministry of Housing, 
Communities and Local Government within 7 days of approving their Council 
Tax requirements.

this is the case, the following financial position is achieved:-

12. Risk Management 
12.1 The management of risk is strategically driven by the Corporate Risk 
Management Group. This is the central hub of a number of council 
mechanisms for risk management including a Corporate Key Strategic Risk 
Register, departmental risk registers and project risk registers. The group 
collates on a quarterly basis the headline departmental risks and planned 
mitigation activity from each department, project and partnership, to be put 
forward for discussion at CLT. Reports on risk are made through the 
Standards and General Purposes Committee to Cabinet and Council. 
 
12.2 Currently c. £6m of savings are being progressed for 2025-26 coupled with a 
further c. £13m of additional savings identified for 2026-30. Current practice is 
to monitor delivery of savings as part of budget monitoring.

13.2 Future Years

• Savings – reduction/deletion of services 
• Savings – efficiencies including procurement 
• Income – increase in fees and charges/new sources of

13.3 Peopletoo were commissioned to undertake a Financial Healthcheck for 
Merton Council. This report forms the first output of this Financial 
Healthcheck, and aims to understand, validate, and assure the Council is 
delivering good value for money services, and outline key success factors to 
inform the Council’s next phase of improvement. 
 
14. Chief Financial Officer Section 25 Statement 
 
Section 25 of the Local Government Act 2003 requires the Chief Financial Officer of 
an authority to report on: 
 
a. the robustness of the estimates made for the purposes of the 
calculations, and 
b. the adequacy of the proposed financial reserves. 
 
In doing so, the Executive Director, Finance and Digital, has had particular regard to 
the guidance offered by the Chartered Institute of Public Finance and Accountancy 
and the views of the Council’s external auditor. 
 
Financial resilience and demonstrating that the Council is a going concern have 
been important considerations in developing the budget for 2026/27 and MTFS 
2026-30. The following paragraphs draw out the key issues and actions that have 
been taken and risks that remain. 
Context and Risks 
 
Fair funding 2.0 
 
There have been many changes to the way that the LGF settlement is presented due 
to the Fair Funding Review, including changes to the Business Rates system. The 
Fair Funding Review has resulted in some major changes to local authorities grant 
shares, both positively and negatively. Based on the review, Merton comes out as a 
relatively low needs, high resourced authority and the settlement reflects that. 
Merton’s grant reduces from £84.216m under the legacy system to £82.022m in 
2026/27, £76.391m in 2027/28, and £70.836m in 2028/29, creating a cumulative loss 
of £13.380m once fully implemented. 
 
This report and previous MTFS updates set out in depth the calculations and factors 
driving the impact on Merton. These include the reduction of our relative needs share 
driven largely from the changes to the Children’s formula, with the impact of our 
relative resources impacted by the assumptions around Council Tax discussed 
further below. 
 
The Council joined London Councils in lobbying around these key points, as well as 
advocating for deprivation to include the impact of housing costs. Whilst deprivation 
was lobbied for successfully and has increased the funding into London, other areas 
were limited in their lobbying impact and the settlement for Merton reflects this. 

=== PDF PAGE 33 ===
30  
The net impact of Fair Funding 2.0 represents a significant financial challenge to the 
authority and when added to the forward forecast of costs and other income leaves a 
further £23.9m to find by 2029/30. The Council will be required to continue delivering 
savings at pace and maintain a strong grip on financial management to ensure 
sustainability within the Medium-Term Financial Strategy. 
 
Although a balanced budget is presented for 2026/27, the impact of Fair Funding 2.0 
will require a major programme of strategic change of transformation and service 
delivery to operate within the renewed financial envelope. This will involve 
exploitation of technology, further commercialisation and strategic change to the way 
the Council operates and delivers services. Given the levels of savings to be 
found it will also inevitably involve some difficult choices and reviews of service 
delivery and standards. It will remain imperative that the Council delivers multi-year 
programmes of change and transformation to meet the savings needed. However 
this is done from a foundation of a strong track-record of financial stability, wise 
investment of legacy funding and low debt and consistent delivery of savings. 
 
Council Tax 
 
This Local Government Finance Settlement has been based on a major review of all 
local authorities’ needs and resources and specifically includes the assumption 
that councils increase their council tax by the maximum permitted under Referendum 
Principles. This report sets out that government has set a notional Council Tax level 
at the average Band D level of Council Tax in England for authorities in scope of 
these reforms. This means that for 2026-27 the notional council tax level used is 
£2,063 and increasing annually after that. Merton’s average Band D council tax in 
2025/26 is £1,604.05 i.e. some £459 below the amount assumed by Government. 
The decision proposed to freeze the level of general Council Tax produces a 
permanent real terms loss in Council Tax yield of up to £3.8m. This would be a 
permanent and compounding loss of funding for the Council budget. Additionally, this 
may may have implications for the council’s future “funding floor” going forward, as 
set out in this report. 
 
Although the foregoing of this income is material, the Council has taken this decision 
to reduce (in real terms) the financial cost of its services to residents and has found 
compensating reductions/service changes which generate the equivalent permanent 
on-going savings, albeit not accounting for the compounding effect on the growth of 
the Council Tax base. This is likely to mean that the challenge of meeting the 
savings required to balance the MTFS will increase as further savings 
become more challenging. This is mitigated through the high percentage of savings 
delivery this Council has a track record of. When making decisions on setting 
Council Tax levels there is also a need to give due regard to the cost of living crisis 
faced by residents. 
 
 
Special Educational Needs and Disabilities (SEND) Reform 
A significant financial challenge for all local authorities is the Dedicated Schools 
Grant (DSG) High Needs budget which, for Merton, has been in deficit since 

=== PDF PAGE 34 ===
31  
2018/19. The government introduced a statutory override to separate DSG deficits 
from broader council finances, extended until March 2028, which provides an 
accounting solution to the problem but does not solve the underlying funding 
problem. The Office for Budget Responsibility estimate nationally that by the end of 
2027-28 the cumulative deficit nationally will be £4.9bn. 
 
Merton joined the Department for Education’s High Needs Safety Valve programme 
in 2022 due to its significant deficit. The Safety Valve programme for Merton enters 
its final year for Merton in 2026/27. 
 
The cumulative DSG deficit at the end of 2024/25 was £53.556m and by the end of 
2025/26 as per December’s forecast will increase to £68.863m which is an increase 
of £0.560m since November 2025. Whilst the actions from the safety valve 
agreement are being carried out, they are not yet resulting in the financial outcomes 
required and present a significant risk to the Council’s financial position over the 
medium-term and an inherent danger to its reserves position with the deficit forecast 
to exceed available reserves. 
 
On 11 June 2025, as part of the Spending Review, the government confirmed that its 
intended approach to SEND reform would be set out in a Schools White Paper in 
autumn 2025. This was subsequently delayed to 2026 with further clarity outlined in 
the Final Local Government Settlement. In light of this planned review the Council 
previously determined it will no longer be earmarking reserves to match the full 
amount of the deficit over and above what is already provided for in the MTFS from 
2025-26 onwards to lessen the strain on the General Fund. 
This approach has and will impact the cashflow position of the Council as the 
deficit continues to grow. Coupled with the impact of the major capital schemes 
outlined in this report this will accelerate the need to borrow over the MTFS 
period with corresponding impact on debt servicing charges to the General 
Fund. Projections are incorporated as part of the Treasury Management Strategy. 
 
While the continuing positive efforts to manage the DSG deficit need to go 
further and deeper, it is clear that once the override ends in 2027/28 the level of 
forecast deficit will exceed the Council’s available revenue resources and it will not 
be able to meet its financial obligations from 2028/29 without reform to the SEND 
system. Many other councils are in a similar position. 
 
As part of the Final Settlement the Government addressed the growing SEND 
deficits and announced a significant funding package to tackle 90 per cent of the 
historic high-needs deficits on the DSG stating that all local councils with a SEND 
deficit will be eligible to receive grant funding, subject to securing approval of a 
SEND local reform plan. This will be in the form of a High Needs Stability Grant. 
Further, the final settlement set out that for 2026/27 and 2027/28 there would be a 
similar programme to tackle the accruing deficit for those financial years, to be 
confirmed at a later date. 
These announcements are welcome and provides a major investment into local 
authorities of circa £5bn and is particularly needed here at Merton given the size of 
our deficit. Alongside wider SEND reforms planned later in the year; this should 

=== PDF PAGE 35 ===
32  
provide a solution to the unsustainable SEND system which has been financially 
challenging for Local Authorities for many years. 
Finally, the Government announced the end of Safety Valve as at the end of 2025/26 
as part of the changes, therefore Merton will no longer be in the scheme, albeit 
2026/27 was the final year for this Council. All of these measures announced are 
subject to conditions, eligibility of spend and approval of a SEND local reform plan. 
Updates will be provided throughout the year. 
 
Adult Social Care, Integrated Care, and Public Health 
 
Across England and London, Merton also continues to experience growing 
pressures within Adult Social Care (ASC), driven by increased demand, more 
complex needs, and rising service costs. This year to date we have seen a rise in 
package of care by 5%. To manage pressures, ASC has put in place comprehensive 
measures which focus on: 
• Managing rising demand through a strength-based approach, enabling 
residents to maintain independence for longer in line with legislation. 
• Preventing escalation of need by investing in early intervention 
and community-based support. 
• Improving commissioning to ensure better value and reduce reliance on 
expensive care packages. 
• Supporting recruitment and retention to stabilise the workforce amidst a 
national shortage of qualified staff. 
• Using technology and digital tools, including portals and smart notes, to 
improve efficiency and support case management. 
These efforts are designed to ensure ASC remains financially sustainable 
while maintaining quality of care for residents. However, each year this becomes 
more challenging. The Council eagerly awaits wider reforms of social care as part of 
the Casey review. Additionally, the reform of local health structures will be key to 
understand the impact on the department and is a key strategic risk. 
 
Children’s Social Care 
 
Merton’s children’s services remain high performing, with its Children’s Services 
having been rated Outstanding again by Ofsted in 2025. But it faces growing 
demand and cost pressures, particularly around complex needs requiring residential 
placements and meeting the challenges for children with enduring health needs. To 
manage this demand, the Directorate is: 
• Investing in the Family First Partnership Programme, which focuses on 
early help, family support, and reducing the need for costly interventions. 
• Strengthening commissioning of children’s placements, expanding local 
provision, and reducing reliance on high-cost external to 
Merton placements. 
• Working strategically with partners, including the ICB and other health 
providers, education, and voluntary sector organisations, to provide 
coordinated support for children and young people. 
• Revisiting the Looked After Children’s Sufficiency Strategy 
• Launching the CLLF recruitment campaign to reduce agency spend 

=== PDF PAGE 36 ===
33  
• Commissioning independent expertise through the company ‘C3’ to 
further progress CLLF transformation 
 
These actions aim to reduce pressures on children’s social care, ensure earlier and 
more effective family support, and maintain high quality safeguarding across the 
borough. However, the financial risks in this area remain significant and volatile 
especially with the impact of the costs passed on by local providers. These 
areas must continue to be managed effectively. 
 
As mentioned earlier, wider SEND reforms will be a key area to understand the 
impact on Merton, as well as wider legislative changes. As with Adults Social Care, 
the reform of local health structures will be imperative to understand the impact on 
the department and Council and is a key strategic risk. 
 
Temporary Accommodation 
 
The total number of placements in temporary accommodation at the beginning of 
April 2025 was 700 compared to 539 one year earlier. The increase in numbers over 
the past year was on average 30%. This is now the largest spend pressure of the 
Council. In last year’s MTFS, a £3.5m recurrent provision was made to invest in 
spending pressures in this area. A further £3m is being made available from 2026/27 
on a permanent basis. This is in addition to £1.2m permanent growth to 
support restructuring the department to deliver on its priorities, including delivering 
the Council’s comprehensive Temporary Accommodation (TA) Cost Reduction 
Programme. 
 
The Council has a strong record of preventing homelessness (60% success rate, 
compared to the London average of 40%). With this investment coupled with the 
recovery plan and availability of contingency resources, it is crucial that the plans 
deliver. TA issues are prevalent across London and major urban areas and, given. 
major risk it presents for the Council will continue to be closely monitored. 
 
Housing 
 
As outlined in other reports in this financial year the Council is embarking on Council 
House building for the first time in a generation. Additionally, there is a programme to 
take further opportunities to develop acquire social housing, as well as purchasing 
homes for temporary accommodation. This programme of activity is forecast to have 
a significantly positive impact on public health outcomes and in turn a reduction of 
spend on associated support services and costs of temporary accommodation. 
 
However, this programme of work does carry risks, particularly exposure to 
construction cost volatility and setting up structures to manage said properties and 
programme-manage builds and acquisitions. It is crucial that governance 
processes and mitigation programmes continue to be robust. Additionally, the 
creation of the Housing Revenue Account (HRA) will require careful planning. 
Proposed growth allocated to the service will support these endeavours. 

=== PDF PAGE 37 ===
34  
Borrowing 
 
Merton has a very low level of debt for a borough of its size and complexity. As of the 
time of this report, £38m of debt exists. This is as a direct result of the sale of the 
Council’s wholly owned company CHAS for £186m, with an element used to pay off 
the Council’s PWLB debt and significant amount invested for long-term 
returns. Additionally, the Council has carried low levels of debt. This sound financial 
management of the Council’s resources has benefited the General Fund greatly, 
with a reduced cost of financing the capital programme and investment returns into 
the MTFS. 
 
With the acceleration of the Council’s house building/acquisition and regeneration 
plans, as well as the impact of the DSG on cash balances, it is inevitable that the 
Council will need to borrow in the term of this MTFS and manage the 
associated debt servicing charges. 
 
Contingencies 
 
More contingencies have been foregone to ensure that structural budget pressures 
have been tackled, with the aim to reduce the reliance of those budgets to offset 
departmental overspends. Further growth has again been included into services 
to ensure departmental budgets are adequate. However, that does 
mean that those contingency budgets have reduced. Although the recurrent 
contingency budgets remain adequate, even at a reduced level to be available for 
contingent events during 2026/27 and onwards, there is a need 
to continually monitor levels as part of the ongoing and regular review 
of the MTFS. Over the MTFS, due to the healthy reserves position the Council holds, 
there is likely to be adequate resourcing available to manage any contingent events 
that may occur. 
 
Wider risks 
 
• Economic Uncertainty: The wider economic environment, including inflation, 
interest rates, and energy prices, creates financial challenges for both the 
Council and residents. 
• Workforce Pressures: Recruitment and retention of skilled staff, particularly in 
specialist roles, continues to be an area requiring close management. 
• Climate and Environmental Risks: The Council is committed to climate action, 
but this brings both cost and opportunity. 
These risks are mitigated through a range of management actions, including active 
budget monitoring, scenario planning, and the maintenance of reserves at a prudent 
level. The Council also has a strong culture of in-year corrective action, ensuring that 
pressures are tackled early and recurrently. 

=== PDF PAGE 38 ===
35  
Robustness of estimates 
 
Forward projections on funding, pay and inflation are challenging in this current 
environment, with macroeconomic and geopolitical events having the potential to 
cause significant volatility to key assumptions. Local government funding 
settlements returning to multi-year settlements going forward are positive and will aid 
financial planning over the medium-term, albeit with a significant reduction in grant 
funding. 
 
Key assumptions made for the MTFS, taking into account expert forecasts and 
internal assessments are shown as below-: 
 
Key Assumptions-: 
 
 2026/27 2027/28 2028/29 2029/30 
Pay inflation (%) 3.0% 2.0% 2.0% 2.0% 
Price inflation (%) 3.0% 2.0% 2.0% 2.0% 
Council Tax increase - General 0.0% 2.0% 2.0% 2.0% 
Council Tax increase – ASC precept 2.0% 2.0% 2.0% 2.0% 
 
 
Further capacity to manage risk is contained within ongoing contingency and general 
inflation budgets, as well as allocated reserves. 
 
Income to the Council has also been challenged through the wider economic 
pressures and the legacy of slow recovery post-covid. The Council now publishes a 
register of all fees and charges annually with a review of changes into the 
next financial year. Further development of the Council’s commercial programme is 
envisaged to aid the council’s financial resilience. 
 
The Council must have regard to the major risks to its financial position and in 
particular: 
 
• Maintaining effective management and delivery of services at a time of 
escalating price inflation 
• The current economic position including the cost-of-living crisis 
and volatile levels of prices inflation. 
• The envisaged financial implications of exiting our PFI arrangements in less 
than 4 years which will have a medium-term call on reserves to manage the 
transition. 
• Maintaining robust and effective budget setting and monitoring 
processes demand pressures on the budget 
• Delivering against the Council’s priorities 
• Identifying and achieving cost and income improvements 
• Managing the decrease in funding over the MTFS period driven by the Fair 
Funding Review 2.0, ensuring a robust programme of transformation is in 
place over this period. 

=== PDF PAGE 39 ===
36  
• The ongoing impact of changing working practices and impact on income 
generation continuing since the pandemic. 
Savings proposals included in this report and in previous reports for implementation 
over the MTFS period have been reviewed and challenged in terms of deliverability 
and have impact assessments included in this pack. Merton has a good track 
record of savings delivery and currently forecasts 89% delivery for this year. It is 
imperative this focus on delivery is maintained over the MTFS period with clear 
savings delivery plans in place. 
 
Reserves 
The forecast balance of earmarked reserves at year-end 2025/26 is £84.906m. At 
the end of the MTFS period 2029-30 it is forecast to be £81.893m. The General 
reserve is £14m which represents a sufficient balance for the level of risk the council 
faces and the size of the budget. 
Reserves are considered healthy, and as of CIPFA’s most 
recent Resilience index date (outlined in this report) show levels present positively 
when measured against statistically similar authorities and represent low 
risk. Furthermore, the Council does not rely on reserves to set a balanced budget. 
 
The forecast the forecast budget monitoring position for 2025/26 is showing a 
£0.785m overspend as at period 9, but with further measures of mitigations it is 
expected the outturn is near to on-budget, subject to any significant emerging cost 
pressures. 
 
This is compared to an overspend last year 
of £2.521m which was ultimately taken from earmarked reserves at year 
end. The strong financial performance in 2025/26 has been as a result 
of robust financial management and investment of growth into service 
pressures. This MTFS outlines further measures to invest back into services not only 
for budget pressures but growth to support the council’s housing and regeneration 
programme, as well as investing into the digital team to drive forward future 
transformation. 
 
It is clear that wider service pressures do remain, as set out in this statement and 
with further savings to identify and deliver there is strong financial management 
required to mitigate the risk of overspending. Close financial monitoring and strong 
preventative action will continue. 
 
As referred to previously, it is clear that without changes to the SEND system, the 
size and worsening position of the DSG represents an inherent danger to the 
Council’s earmarked reserve balances and presents a significant risk to the Council’s 
financial position in the medium term. With current projections on the DSG deficit, at 
the expiration of the accounting override at the end of 2027/28, reserves will not be 
sufficient in 2028/29 to cover the deficit that will be accrued. However, the 
announcement in the Final Settlement of a High Needs Stability Grant is positive and 
welcome and should be beneficial to the Council, but further clarity is awaited in 

=== PDF PAGE 40 ===
37  
2026/27 of Merton’s allocation, treatment of unfunded elements of the deficit and the 
treatment of deficits up until the override. 
 
This, coupled with the Council’s efforts to be effective in implementing its plans at 
managing its overspend position and tackling the DSG deficit, alongside a 
government review of the DSG being positive for local authorities, the Council has 
sufficient reserves over the life of the MTFS having eliminated any dependency on 
them to balance the budget. 
 
The Council’s reserves and their usage is linked to both the capital and revenue 
budget. A list of the current reserves held by the Council is attached at Appendix 8. 
 
Taking into account this MTFS report and assessment of risk set out in this 
statement , the levels of reserves are sufficient to provide a reasonable level of 
mitigation for the risks that the council faces over the medium- 
term and are currently adequate for 2026/27. 
Governance and Financial Management Framework 
Merton Council operates within a robust framework of financial management and 
internal control. Standing orders, financial regulations, and codes of practice guide 
financial decision-making at all levels. Regular in-year budget monitoring, including 
reporting to Cabinet and scrutiny committees, ensures transparency and 
accountability. The Council has a well-established internal audit function and benefits 
from external challenge through its appointed auditors and sector peers. 
 
Monitoring arrangements are comprehensive, and variances are reported promptly. 
Corrective actions are identified and implemented by managers with oversight from 
the Corporate Management Team. This ongoing scrutiny is vital to the continued 
robustness of the Council’s financial position and underpins my confidence in both 
the estimates and the wider financial strategy. 
 
Summary 
Chief Officers have reviewed their budgets in line with departmental finance officers 
and are confident that the budgets set for their services are robust and, together with 
the corporate contingency, will meet the strategic 
objectives and performance targets set out in this MTFS. Further, sufficient controls 
and governance is in place to effectively monitor and manage the Council’s 
finances. 
 
In summary, it is the view of the Chief Financial Officer (being the Executive Director, 
Finance and Digital) that the estimates are robust for the purposes of the required 
budget/council tax calculations for 2026/27 and that the Council’s reserves are 
adequate in setting the budget for 2026/27.

Building a Better Merton Together 
Merton as a borough has a huge amount  to offer those who live , work or visit here.  It is one 
of the greenest  boroughs in London and has plenty of parks, green  spaces  and rivers offe 
ring easy access to nature. It is extremely well connected to central London, as we ll as 
neighbouring boroughs and the Surrey  Hills. Each year, 500,000 visitors come to our 
borough to enjoy the Wimbledon Championships , which have been  held since 1877 at the 
All-England Club. Merton is also proud to be the home once more of AFC Wimble don in 
the Plough Lane stadium. 
We are a growing and diverse borough – Merton is home to 215,187 people, an increase 
of 7% over the last 10 years,  and the population is made up of a broad range of diffe rent 
communitie s. One third of residents were born outside the UK and the most common 
languages spoken  after English are Polis h, Tamil, Portuguese  and Urdu, which reflects 
the Polis h, European, South American and Asian communitie s that have made Me rton the 
ir home.  
We have always strived for Merton to be a great  place to raise a family. There is e xce llent 
support  for childre n and young people and almos t all our schools are rated either good or 
outstanding. We are one of the safest  boroughs in London, and we have worked hard to 
prevent homelessness  and maintain the lowest number  of households in temporary 
accommodation  of all London’s boroughs. However, we know there is more to do to make 
Merton a better place to live for e ve ryone. 
If we want to achie ve the goal of nurturing civic pride in Merton, this must be truly fe lt and 
experienced  by all communitie s across  the borough. Parts of Merton have highe r levels of 
overcrowding, poorer  health, deprivation and poverty and a greater  proportion of lower-
income households that are more affected by the ris ing cost of living. We will target  our 
delivery to address  these disparities and to make sure that investment, support, and action 
goes  to the right places and makes a difference. 
We have shown that we work best  when we work together and that will become  even more 
important in the years ahead.  The council cannot  achie ve these  ambitions alone but will 
need  to work closely and collaborative ly with communitie s, businesses,  and partners on a 
local and London-wide le ve l to build a better Merton together.  
Councillor Ross Garrod, Leader, Merton Council 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Councillor Stephen Alambritis MBE, Lead Member Finance & Corporate Services

The Capital Strategy is an integral part of the Council’s Strategic and Financial Planning 
Framework: 
 
The Corporate Plan is specific to Merton Council and sets out the council’s vision and 
ambitions for improvement over the next four years and how this will be achieved. Corporate 
Planning and financial planning frameworks are closely aligned and integrated. 
The Medium Term Financial Strategy (MTFS) is a 4 year plan which sets out our commitment 
to provide services that meet the needs of people locally, and represents good value for 
money. It links our council vision and priorities with forecasted resources and budgets. This 
shows how our finances will be structured and managed to ensure they support our priorities, 
and those of our partners. It incorporates the medium term impact on rate payers of activity 
within both the Capital Strategy and the Treasury Management Strategy Statement. 
Capital Strategy - The importance of having a meaningful and comprehensive Capital 
Strategy is recognised by the Chartered Institute of Public Finance and Accountancy’s 
(CIPFA) in both its Prudential Code (2021) and Capital Strategy Guidance. It provides a high 
level overview of how capital expenditure, capital financing and treasury management activity 
contributes to the provision of services, along with an overview of how associated risk is 
managed and what the implications might be for future financial sustainability. 
Treasury Management Strategy Statement (TMSS) summarises the management of the 
council’s cash flows, its banking, money market and capital market transactions and the 
effective control of the risks associated with those activities and the pursuit of optimum 
performance consistent with those risks.

change programme and corporate items:

Capital Strategy 
The Council recognises the vital contribution its Capital Strategy plays in the economic 
growth of the borough. The significant and strategically planned investment shows that 
Merton is an ambitious borough looking to invest in improvements of placemaking to create 
an economic resilience which allows both local business and communities to thrive. It also 
welcomes the opportunity to work with the private sector to deliver its priorities and for the 
private sector to see Merton as a place it wants to invest in. The Council recognises that it 
needs to leverage private investment to create a resilient local economy and deliver the best 
outcomes for residents and businesses. 
In developing the Capital Strategy, clear linkages have also been identified with not only the 
Corporate Plan, MTFS and TMS but departmental service and commissioning plans beneath 
this. It reflects the capital investment implications of the approved objectives of those plans, 
which themselves reflect the council’s proposals set out in service based strategies such as 
the Primary Places Strategy, Local Implementation Plan (Transport), and Asset Management 
Plans.

Evidence of Need 
Merton is an outer London borough. The 2021 Census data shows Merton’s resident 
population as 215,200 (which is the actual number as opposed to a projection from 
previous years’ data). In 2022 Merton had a resident population of 205,820 according to 
the housing led projections which is predicted to increase to 214,999 by 2035 
 
According to the 2021 Census Merton has a higher number of young working age 
population (73,500) compared to the housing led projection (64,958) 
The number of usual residents in the Merton population by age groups. Source: Office for 
National Statistics, Census Data 2021. 
 
The Chart over the page represents the numbers of people by age group and East/West 
Merton as well as the projected numbers by 2035. Currently, East Merton has a younger 
population and a higher number of residents in the 25-44 year old working age population 
compared to West Merton with an older population and higher numbers of residents in the 
65 year and over population. By 2035 projections indicate that East Merton will continue to 
have higher numbers in the younger working age population compared to West Merton, 
but numbers will be balanced in the older age group population between East and West 
Merton. 
 
The population of younger age groups (0-17 year olds) is projected to decrease from 
48,806 in 2022 to 41,950 by 2035, the working age groups (25-64 years) is projected to 
increase from 116,782 in 2022 to 119,655 by 2035 and the population of older age (65 
years and over) is projected to increase from 27,645 in 2022 to 37,713 by 2035. 

=== PDF PAGE 47 ===
44 
 
 
Numbers of residents in East and West Merton by age group in 2022 including 2035 projections, Merton 
 
 
By 2035, the largest increases in the numbers of residents can be seen generally in the 
older age groups, whereas children and young people aged 5-17 years will decrease. 
Local research supports the increasing need identified in the London Plan for housing 
designed for older people, including sheltered and extra-care. 
The Local Plan sets out that the council will plan for 11,732 new homes over the 15 year 
plan period from 2021 to 2036. Delivery of this housing is required to meet the needs of 
the increase in population and growth; projected to be an overall increase of approximately 
16,600 people. 
The Infrastructure Delivery Plan identifies the anticipated strategic requirements for the 
provision of a range of different infrastructure types across the borough. These include 
transport, health, education, green and blue infrastructure, sport and leisure, community, 
utilities, climate change, economic development and emergency services. 
The council must ensure that the necessary infrastructure required to support new 
residential and commercial development throughout the borough can be delivered at the 
right time, therefore addressing any existing shortfalls in provision. 
We have co-designed a methodology with our partners to improve how we work with our 
local communities and use local, council and partner assets and resources. This work was 
undertaken with Collaborate CIC, one of the leaders in the field of collaboratively designed 
public services and produced ‘Working Better with Communities’ framework. This model 
takes what we learned from how we worked differently with partners during the pandemic, 
feedback from community groups and best practice from Merton and elsewhere to offer a 
guide to how we can work differently with communities. 
 
The commissioning of our new multi-million-pound Civic Pride Fund: Supporting the 
Voluntary and Community Sector has provided a valuable opportunity to this put this 
framework into practice. The fund invests in social infrastructure at a neighbourhood level 
and supports our voluntary groups.
