 ASCIC&PH 
137 
Service/Section Maximising Independence by adopting a strategic approach to Supported Living 60,134 215 0 0 0 Low Low SP1 N N/A 
Description This proposal aims to expand opportunities for independent living and reduce reliance on residential and institutional care by better aligning our existing 
supported accommodation with the changing needs of people with learning disabilities (LD), mental health conditions (MH), and older adults. Our focus is on 
increasing independence by enabling people to live as independently as possible, for as long as possible, within their own communities. 
 
We will review and compare the evolving needs of our residents against the current accommodation and support offer to identify gaps and opportunities. This 
will inform future commissioning plans and support social work teams to promote greater choice, control, and personalised care. By doing so, we can reduce 
admissions to long-term institutional settings and support people to remain in more appropriate, community-based environments. This approach supports the 
NHS 10-Year Plan’s ambition to shift care from hospitals into the community and aligns with the opportunities presented by the proposed Integrated 
Neighbourhood Teams (INTs) in working closer to health colleagues. This proposal aims to increase opportunities for independent living, maximising 
independence and reduce residential placements by aligining our existing accommodation with support services to meet the changing needs of people with 
LD, MH and older people. 
 
Service Implication 
 
Continue to ensure the efficient use of accommodation with support services 
Staffing Implications None 
Strategic Priorities 
implications 
Sustainability, better use of resources

 ASCIC&PH 
138 
Service/Section Using predictive technology to maximise independence, manage demand and fulfil our statutory duties (ASCIPPH 128 Extension) 60,134 500 0 0 0 Low Low SP1 N N/A 
 
Description Artificial Intelligence (AI), particularly predictive AI, is advancing at an unprecedented pace. Improvements in data processing, machine learning algorithms, 
and real-time analytics are enabling AI to make increasingly accurate predictions about health and behavior. These rapid developments mean AI tools are 
becoming more reliable and accessible, offering powerful new ways to support early intervention, personalized care, and better decision-making in health and 
social care settings.This proposal outlines our proactive approach to managing increasing and more complex demand on Adult Social Care. By integrating 
smart technology and predictive artificial intelligence, we aim to support people to live independently for longer, reduce avoidable hospital admissions, and 
deliver more targeted, preventative care. This aligns with the NHS 10-Year Plan, the development of Integrated Neighbourhood Teams (INT), and supports 
the broader shift toward community-based, digitally enabled health and care services. Adult Social care is experiencing an increase of demand to its front 
door which includes a higher number of cases which are more complex. This is not only due to an ageing population, but people are living longer and 
therefore their needs are becoming more complex. 
 
With this increase in demand within Adult Social Care, it is critical that this is managed effectively. Not only to ensure we undertake our statutory duties to give 
the support to those most vulnerable residents and their carers, but also to ensure our resources and budget are used effectively and equitably. 
 
We have been working on increasing the use of smart technology to keep people independent and at home for longer for some time now which has proven 
successful. Therefore, we aim to extend this by using a newly commissioned Artificial Intelligence (AI) tool which can predict changes in behaviours to identify 
risk. Identifying risks such as falls or UTI’s will allow us to implement targeted prevention support to residents to mitigate the risks and delay the need for long 
term care and support from both health and social care. Implementing this predictive technology will enable the council to undertake our Adult Social care 
statutory duties and prevent, reduce and delay need for our residents. 
 
This strategy aligns to the NHS 10-year plan and the proposed development of Integrated Neighbourhood Teams (INT) and closer working with health 
partners. It supports the shift from hospital to community as well as the NHS digital transformation. 
 
 
 
Service Implication 
 
 
We are supporting this by implementing a targeted review team within the service. 
Staffing Implications None 
Strategic Priorities 
implications 
Sustainability, better use of resources 
Impact on other 
depts 
None

Implications 
None

 ASCIC&PH 
139 
Service/Section A review of residents receiving Section 117 aftercare to determine current needs, ongoing eligibility, and whether the support in place remains 
appropriate and proportionate in line with legistlation. 
60,134 435 0 0 0 Low Low SP1 N N/A 
Description People who have been sectioned under the Mental Health Act 1983, often face complex and ongoing vulnerabilities when leaving hospital, including mental 
health challenges and difficulties reintegrating into the community. Regularly reviewing their aftercare under Section 117 is crucial to ensure they continue to 
receive the right level of support tailored to their changing needs. These reviews provide an opportunity to promote greater independence, helping individuals 
regain control over their lives, reduce reliance on institutional care, and improve their overall wellbeing. Section 117 of the Mental Health Act means that when 
someone has been detained in hospital for treatment under certain sections (like section 3), they are entitled to free aftercare once they leave hospital. This 
includes things like help with housing, healthcare, or social support, and it is provided by both the NHS and local authorities. Targeted reviews with people in 
receipt of S117 will be undertaken to determine continued eligibility for S117 services and ensure they receive the right support plan. A review of the existing 
funding split with health will also be undertaken to include potential eligibility for Continuing Health Care (CHC), and opportunities to further promote the 
person’s independence. Targeted S117 reviews will take place with the person and undertaken jointly with health. The ending of the S75 agreement in 
October 2024 and the return of the MH social work service to ASC enables us to focus on conducting S117 reviews in a more timely way and to use a 
strengths based approach in doing so to promote a person’s independence. 
 
 
Service Implication 
 
 
To scope the number of targetted reviews and provide SW capacity to undertake these as a priority to work with health professionals involved in these joint 
reviews. To ensure that the S117 protocol for SWL that is being refined will support the aims and objectives of these reviews. 
 
Staffing Implications 
 
Identify dedicated Social Work capacity to undertake this work. 
Strategic Priorities 
implications 
This supports the Council's priority on Sustainable Communites. 
Impact on other 
depts 
Reviews may identify need for supported housing 
Equalities 
Implications 
EIA to be completed, but no immediate equalities implications due to the individual strength based approach to be used. 
 ASCIC&PH 
140 
Service/Section Streamling Financial Assessment Processes (8,424) 275 0 0 0 Medium Medium SP1 Y Service Users 
Description Under the Care Act 2014, local authorities are permitted to charge individuals for the care and support they receive, subject to a financial assessment. 
However, delays in completing these assessments often result in a gap between the start of services and the issuing of accurate charges. This can lead to lost 
or delayed income, increased debt, and confusion or dissatisfaction for residents. Timely and accurate financial assessments are essential to ensure Adult 
Social Care (ASC) services are appropriately funded and that residents contribute fairly to their care costs. 
 
To address these challenges, we propose streamlining the financial assessment process. This includes introducing digital self-assessment tools, automating 
workflows, and improving early engagement with residents to set clear expectations around charging. Better integration between care management and 
finance teams will ensure assessments are initiated promptly, while enhanced debt recovery processes will support earlier intervention and clearer 
communication. These changes are expected to accelerate income collection, reduce outstanding debt, and improve resident experience 
 
 
Service Implication 
 
We aim to improve the financial assessment process in Adult Social Care by streamlining workflows, introducing digital tools, and enhancing early 
engagement with residents — enabling faster income collection, reducing debt, and improving overall efficiency and resident experience. 
Staffing Implications None 
Strategic Priorities 
implications 
Building a Suatainable Future 
Impact on other 
depts 
None 
Equalities 
Implications 
Yes

 ASCIC&PH 
141 
Service/Section Transforming commissioning in Adult Social Care to deliver better outcomes for residents 60,134 325 0 0 0 Low Low SP1 N N/A 
Description Adult social care commissioning is the process by which local authorities plan, purchase, and oversee care and support services to meet the needs of 
residents. It involves assessing local demand, selecting and working with providers, managing contracts, and ensuring services are high quality, cost-effective, 
and promote independence and wellbeing. 
 
Under the Care Act 2014, local authorities have a legal duty to commission services that: 
 
Promote individual wellbeing 
Prevent or delay the development of care needs 
Provide accessible information and advice 
Shape a diverse, sustainable care market that offers choice and quality 
 
In Merton, we are transforming our commissioning approach to better reflect these duties and respond to the changing landscape of health and care. This 
includes aligning with the NHS Long Term Plan and the evolving role of Integrated Care Boards (ICBs). 
 
Key elements of our transformation include: 
 
Adopting data-led, place-based approaches to better understand and respond to local needs 
Strengthening partnerships across health, care, and the voluntary sector to deliver integrated, person-centred support 
Reviewing existing contracts to ensure they remain fit for purpose and deliver value for money 
Introducing more flexible commissioning models that support innovation, responsiveness, and sustainability. 
 
This shift will enable us to commission services that are not only more efficient but also more personalised — supporting residents to live well, with greater 
choice and control over their care. 
 
Service Implication 
 
Under the Care Act 2014, all local authorities have key duties around market management that guide commissioning activity 
Staffing Implications None 
Strategic Priorities 
implications 
Building a Sustainable Future 
Impact on other 
depts 
None 
Equalities 
Implications 
None at this stage, EIAs will be undertaken for each piece if commissioning activity.

 ASCIC&PH 
142 
Service/Section Empowering Residents through Self Directed Support (Direct Payments) 60,134 250 0 0 0 Low Low SP1 N N/A 
Description Direct Payments under the Care Act 2014 are a vital mechanism for enabling personalised care and promoting individual autonomy. They empower residents 
to make choices about how their care is delivered — selecting providers, negotiating terms, and tailoring support to meet their unique needs. This flexibility 
often leads to higher satisfaction and better outcomes, as care is shaped around the person rather than a standardised service model. 
 
Direct Payments also open up access to a wider range of support options, including community-based services, assistive technology, and the employment of 
personal assistants. Carers can benefit too, receiving Direct Payments to support their role, which can help reduce stress and improve overall wellbeing. 
 
Importantly, individuals can manage their allocated budget more flexibly — spending in ways that suit their circumstances or saving for future needs. This 
aligns closely with the Care Act’s emphasis on wellbeing, prevention, and empowerment. 
 
Currently, Merton is in line with the national average for Direct Payment uptake. However, increasing their use presents a clear opportunity to maximise value 
for money in Adult Social Care as well as maximising indepence for residents. 
 
Expanding the use of Direct Payments will help us move away from a one-size-fits-all model and towards a more responsive, person-centred care system — 
one that delivers better outcomes and makes the most of our resources. 
 
 
Service Implication 
 
 
Direct Payments offer greater control to Individuals as they can choose their care provider, negotiate terms, and change arrangements without going through 
a third party. This will mean a focus on that the councils market management duties to ensure a viable market for residnets to purchase from. 
Staffing Implications None 
Strategic Priorities 
implications 
Building a Suatainable Future 
Impact on other 
depts 
None 
Equalities 
Implications 
None

 IC 2026-27 01 Service/Section Marketing and Communications 862 30    Low Low SS1 N Staff 
Description Salary base budget reduction: This reflects 35% of the base salaries for Design Studio staff, funded through existing internal 
recharges, with no impact on other Council budgets. Additional cost avoidance has been achieved by replacing external design 
services with in-house staff. 
Service Implication The Design Studio will continue to provide a full creative and design service to internal clients, with a stronger focus on income 
generation and cost recovery. Service levels will be maintained, and the internal recharging model will promote better visibility of costs 
and more efficient use of internal design resources. There is no reduction in capacity or output anticipated. 
 
Staffing Implications 
 
There are no redundancies or changes to staffing levels as part of this proposal. The existing team will continue to operate under 
current terms and conditions. Staff will, however, work to a more commercially focused model, with an emphasis on client service and 
internal chargeback processes to recover costs. The change supports financial sustainability while maintaining job security. 
 
Strategic Priorities implications 
 
This proposal supports the Council Plan priority Building a Sustainable Future by ensuring that internal services operate eff iciently and 
contribute to the council’s wider financial resilience. It aligns with the Communications and Engagement Strategy’s aim to mo dernise 
operations, promote creative excellence, and provide best value for residents through reduced reliance on external suppliers. 
 
Impact on other departments 
 
There is no direct financial impact on other departments. Internal recharging already forms part of existing practice and wil l continue 
under transparent arrangements. Departments will retain access to high -quality in-house design support, benefiting from a consistent  
corporate brand and reduced external procurement costs. 
 
Equalities Implications An Equality Impact Assessment has been completed and found no negative impact on staff or service users. No posts are being 
removed, and there are no changes to employment conditions or access to services. The proposal supports equality by maintaining 
stable employment and developing commercial and creative skills within the existing workforce.

Description Planning Performance Agreements - Developers are willing to pay for an enhanced planning service which, currently has been for 
major developments. However, the Council is going to seek these for all minor applications except for those from householders. 
This includes those for new residential developments up to 9 units, for smaller scale non-residential developments under 1000 
square metres of floorspace, and for discharge of conditions. 
 
Service Implication 
 
Increased revenue and fewer delays but more pressure on staff for delivery. Greater certainty in the process for developers. 
Staffing Implications If income is not realised, there would be an impact on revenue to cover existing posts. 
Strategic Priorities implications To encourage economic growth and building a sustainable future. 
Impact on other departments A requirement for strengthened collaboration with internal consultees on minor planning applications between Regeneration and 
Development Management. 
Equalities Implications Fees are charged to applicants as a paid for service which would have no adverse implications on the Council's equalities duties to 
residents.

 2026-27 ECPC 03 Service/Section Public Realm 2,544 500    Low Medium SI2 NO N/A 
Description Enhancing the council's statutory Network Management duties to  
keep traffic moving, reduce emissions and to improve road safety by 
enforcing existing traffic regulations more effectively.  
Service Implication The council has statutory Network Management duties to keep traffic 
moving, therefore reducing journey times and emissions, as well as  
improving road safety. The existing camera infrastructure will be  
reviewed so that operational cameras that have resulted in changed  
driver behaviour at some locations can be redeployed to locations  
where enforcement is currently not active. Following traffic surveys, it 
is likely that that the number of active cameras will increase to  
improve compliance with existing traffic regulations in a number of  
locations across the borough.  
Any contraventions at these locations will be captured by the  
cameras and will result in a penalty charge notice being issued to the 
registered driver of the vehicle.  
Delivery of this saving is to be supported by investment in  
enforcement infrastructure, to be managed through the Council’s 
capital programme. 
 
Staffing Implications 
 
There are likely to be greater numbers of penalty charge notices  
generated and this will place some pressure on parking service  
staffing but this will be closely managed.  Additional specialist 
resource will be employed for short period to complete regulatory 
and technical requirements.  
Strategic Priorities 
implications 
None 
Impact on other 
departments 
None 
Equalities 
Implications 
There are no implications for equalities.  All drivers are required to 
adhere to highways legislation and the rules that have been set out 
in the Traffic Signs Regulations 2016

Staffing 
This proposed reorganisation has been developed to deliver improved value for money  
while safeguarding statutory responsibilities and maintaining compliance with Department

This proposal will reorganise the Virtual School, ensuring that the statutory responsibilities 
of the Virtual School Headteacher are maintained, including promoting the education of  
looked-after and previously looked-after children to ensure they have the opportunity to

an over representation of children with additional needs, and children from families living in  
poverty.

staffing will disproportionately affect women.

HC&OP ASCIC&PH143 Service/Section Strengthening Market Sustainability Through Data-Led Inflation Modelling 60,134 238 0 0 0 Low Low SP1 N N/A 
Description This proposal introduces an evidence-led framework for setting annual inflation uplifts for residential, nursing, and 
supported living care providers. In 2025/26, we invested £1 million in inflationary uplifts (excluding home care), 
recognising the financial pressures faced by providers operating within tight margins. The majority of care in Merton 
is delivered by independent and private sector organisations, and it is essential that they remain commercially viable 
to continue delivering safe, high -quality care. The Council has a duty  under the Care Act 2014 to balance the fair 
cost of care with providers’ legitimate need to operate sustainably,  ensuring the market remains resilient,  diverse, 
and capable of meeting the evolving needs of vulnerable adults.  
 
Our approach for 2026/27 will draw on verified data sources —including the Consumer Prices Index (CPI), Adult 
Social Care (ASC) cost data such as the DHSC Market Sustainability and Improvement Fund, and recognised tools 
like CareCubed—to ensure that fee uplifts are fair, transparent, and responsive to local market conditions. This 
proposal strengthens the Council’s Care Act duties, particularly its responsibility to promote a diverse and 
sustainable care market and ensure fair payment for services. We will work closely with co-commissioners, including 
other South West London local authorities and the South West London Integrated Care Board (SWL ICB), to ensure 
consistency and alignment across the sub-region. This approach ensures that inflationary decisions are grounded in 
robust data and market intelligence, safeguarding continuity of care while maintaining financial sustainability and 
equitable commissioning.  It also ensures that financial resources are targeted effectively to meet both statutory 
duties and long-term market sustainability objectives.  
 
Service Implication 
 
Providers will continue to receive inflationary  uplifts, but these will be based on verified evidence and local market 
conditions rather than an across-the-board percentage. The new model strengthens transparency and accountability 
in fee setting, enabling sustainable inflation management without reducing service quality or access. It directly 
supports the Council’s duty to shape and maintain a stable, diverse, and sustainable care market capable of meeting 
the needs of all adults in need of care and support.  
Staffing Implications No direct staffing reductions. Analytical and modelling work will be delivered within existing commissioning and 
finance teams, supported by enhanced use of market and cost data.  
Strategic Priorities 
implications 
Sustainability, better use of resources 
Impact on other 
departments 
The Finance and Procurement teams will support implementation and contract modelling. Legal Services may 
review terms related to fee uplift methodology. No adverse impact on operational services.  
Equalities Implications None 
Total Savings Proposals 2026-30  238 0 0 0

OSC CW202627-S01 Service/Section Council-wide 85,739 1,250 0 0 0 Medium Low SS1 N N/A 
 
Description 
 
Applying a managed  vacancy factor percentage  to pay budgets (excluding  budgets funded by grants, trading accounts  or other 
contributions). The allocation is based on 1.5% of  original pay budgets  
 
Service Implication 
 
This saving will require services  to actively manage their pay budgets based on a reduced allocation by applying a target vacancy  
percentage  to pay budgets of 1% applied across all directorates  pay budgets. Based on analysis of current and previous years' outturn 
of spend against pay budgets  as well as sector vacancy data, this  is a target that should be achievable through normal turnover of  
staff throughout the year, natural post -reductions  from service redesigns and other measures such as  recruitment lags, further  
management  of agency and overtime costs, phased recruitments  and careful review and management  of the need to recruit to vacant 
posts. The average 
rate will account from the variations  of vacancy rates in different departments  and staffing professions. 
 
 
Staffing Implications 
 
Some posts may remain unfilled for part of the year through natural turnover and phased recruitments 
Strategic Priorities implications Building Sustainable  Future – Ensuring the best use of resources,  obtaining value for money and ensuring the best performance  and 
outcomes 
 
 
Impact on other departments 
 
 
All departments  will be affected except where posts are funded by grants and contributions. 
 
 
Equalities Implications 
 
 
None 
OSC CW202627-S02 Service/Section Council-wide 85,739 300 0 0 0 Low Low SS1 N N/A 
Description Review of vacant posts across the council to delete a funded post from each department 
Service Implication There are a number of funded but vacant posts across the council. This process will identify a vacant post from each of the Council's 
6 departments  that can be deleted from the establishment, with a resulting saving.  There may  be service implications, but it is  
anticipated that the impact can be managed.  No services will cease or be significantly  reduced as a result. The EIA will be reviewed 
and updated once the posts are identified.  
Staffing Implications No direct implications  on staff, as these will be unfilled posts. However the impact of deleting posts on teams will need to be managed  
carefully 
Strategic Priorities implications Building Sustainable  Future – Ensuring the best use of resources,  obtaining value for money and ensuring the best performance  and 
outcomes 
Impact on other departments All departments  will be affected as a vacant post will be deleted from all 6 Directorates 
Equalities Implications None

Service/Section Strategic joint commissioning & service delivery for vulnerable children  
27,260 
 
1,580

N/A 
Description At the core of this proposal is the Merton partnerships attempt to achieve a strategic balance between the cost of service  
delivery and maintaining outcomes by building on the joint responsibilities of Education , Adult Social Care specific to 
transitions , the ICB, Public Health and other Health Providers with the specialist support from Channel 3 to implement a  
transformation programme across CLLF. For the first stage of the transformation, a high-level review has been completed on 
the Children’s social care placement budget. Children and young people in Merton’s care access a variety of different types 
of care placements according to their assessed needs and their age.  
 
As part of this proposal CLLF will continue to work with partners to re-baseline the budgets associated with all placements 
and packages of care for children and young people, to ensure more holistic support is tailored across the system.  
 
This will include :- 
 - Work with Riverside to support the joint efforts to meet the needs of children with CHC needs. 
 - Returning in house fostering to a growth model modernizing the recruitment and retention of in-house foster carers and build 
our current Merton fostering community. 
 - Securing local preferred provider commissioning relationships with good and outstanding residential providers with a target 
to ‘soft block’ 4 beds ring fenced to Merton including a step down from Tier 4 or transition into adulthood from the soft block 
service level agreement. 
 - With Housing continue to drive the implementation of Merton’s Homes Project which will support young people to develop 
their independent skills and remain in Merton. This will further strengthen the joint working between Adults Social care, ICB 
and CLLF to ensure effective transition for young people and their families from Children’s Services to Adult Services and  
preparation for adulthood, supporting young people to develop the necessary skills for independent living, employment, good 
health and becoming an active member of the local community without an over reliance on statutory services. This will also 
include maximising Housing benefits. 
Service Implication This will further strengthen the joint working between Adults Social care, ICB and CLLF to ensure effective transition for  
young people and their families from Children’s Services to Adult Services and preparation for adulthood, supporting young 
people to develop the necessary skills for independent living, employment, good health and becoming an active member of 
the local community without an over reliance on statutory services.  
 
Staffing Implications 
 
Council staff delivering this support will be positively affected through the training and development programme that will 
underpin the proposed changes. 
Strategic Priorities 
implications 
Civic Pride 
Impact on other 
departments 
Health partners and internal colleagues including social care and education need to work together. 
Equalities Implications None

Service Implication This additional funding will help our mitigation efforts to address this significant budget pressure, alongside the actions around

  Service/Section Housing & Sustainable Development Staffing and Temporary Accommodation (TA) support 1,238    GS1 
Description An ongoing budget of £1.238m per year is estimated to be required for H&SD as follows: 
1. Creating senior management capacity to deliver the Council's priorities 
2. Create additional capacity within the Housing Needs service to respond to the rise in demand with a focus on reducing the 
number of households in TA, increase the number of housing discharges into permanent accommodation, reduce the use of 
nightly paid TA, reduce the cost of TA and maximise the Housing Benefit recovered  
3. Create additional capacity focusing on the compliance of the Council's property portfolio, develop an asset strategy and 
ensure the Council secures property income from its estate. 
4. Create capacity to continue supporting Town Centres and businesses. 
5. £0.2m of resources in Finance to increase capacity in the Housing Benefits team in order to maximise Housing Benefit  
income to offset temporary accommodation costs incurred by the council. In addition, a dedicated Finance Business Partner to 
the department to support on TA but also focus on the wider Housing and Regeneration programme, both capital and revenue 
and to prepare the Council for the creation of a new Housing Revenue Account (HRA). This will support the increasing workload 
in Housing and Regeneration and it is important to ensure a fully focussed finance resource to support these key Council 
priorities. 
 
Service Implication 
 
Fit for purpose structure to address both senior level and operational capacity to deliver key priorities as set out.

corporate / cross cutting priorities.

Staffing Implications Four additional posts and one co-ordinator 
Strategic Priorities implications Supports community safety, prevention, and safer neighbourhood priorities.

Section F 
 
Risk Management 
Strategy & Policy 
2026/27 


=== PDF PAGE 277 ===
Introduction 
274 
 
 
Organisations exist to achieve their ambitions, aims and objectives. Risk 
Management is the process by which organisations methodically address and identify 
the risks that may prevent them from achieving these ambitions, aims and objectives. 
The intention is to achieve sustained benefit within each of their activities, and across 
the portfolio of all their activities. Ultimately, risk management is about creating a 
better understanding of the most important problems facing organisations. 
Risk is also implicit in the decisions all organisations take; how those decisions are 
taken will affect how successful they are in achieving their objectives. Decision 
making is, in turn, an integral part of the day -to-day existence and is particularly 
significant in times of change. Risk management therefore is a key component in the 
management of change and helps to support effective decision making. 
We endeavour to identify all risks facing the council and to monitor, manage and 
mitigate (where possible) all those risks which are deemed to be high (scored Amber 
or Red). Risks are monitored via Departmental Risk Registers, and key crosscutting 
risks to the council are also placed on the Key Strategic Risk Register (KSRR). 
Merton London Borough Council is committed to effective risk management to 
safeguard public resources, achieve strategic objectives, and deliver quality services. 
This strategy and policy outlines our approach and arrangements to identifying, 
assessing, managing, and monitoring risks, supporting transparency, accountability, 
and resilience. 
Risk management links closely with Health and Safety, Business Continuity, 
Emergency Planning and Insurance; by ensuring close links we can enhance our 
resilience. Generally, a single issue or risk will fall into only one of these categories; 
however some may fall into two or more. As Business Continuity is a way of mitigating 
risk, its link with risk management is key to ensuring the continuous delivery of 
services which are important to the community. 
Figure 1 – Risk Management Structure 
 
 

=== PDF PAGE 278 ===
Policy Statement 
275 
 
 
Merton’s policy is to manage our risks by identifying, assessing and controlling them, 
with the aim of eliminating or reducing them to acceptable levels whilst being mindful 
that some risks will always exist and will never be eliminated. 
 
The council recognises its responsibility to risk management by supporting a 
structured, systematic and focused approach to risk management through the approval 
of our risk management strategy. 
 
The effective management of risk is at the core of our approach to delivering cost 
effective and efficient services as well as sound corporate governance and is a 
continuous and evolving process, running through our strategies and service delivery 
arrangements. As risk is very much concerned with our objectives, the management 
of it will be closely linked to the creation of our strategic, service, project and 
partnership objectives and plans. 
Our risk management process will be continuous and will support internal and 
external change. The risk management process will be fully integrated with the normal 
business management processes across the authority. 
Merton’s aims and objectives in relation to risk management are to: 
• Establish and maintain a robust framework and procedures for the identification, 
analysis, assessment and management of risk, including reporting and recording. 
• Minimise the council’s exposure to unacceptable levels of risk, minimise injury, 
damage, loss and inconvenience to staff, residents and service users. 
• Integrate risk management into the day-to-day activities of staff and the culture of 
the organisation, raising awareness of the importance and need for risk 
management. 
• Assign clear roles and responsibilities for councillors and officers responsible for 
risk management 
• Ensure consistent application of our methodology across all of our activities, 
including partnerships and projects. 
• Effectively manage the total cost of risk. 
 
We will achieve this by: 
• Having a clear and concise risk management strategy which underpins our 
approach and responsibilities to risk 
• Incorporating risk management into business planning, project management and 
service delivery 
• Monitoring risk on a regular basis through the Corporate Risk Management Group 
(CRMG) 
• Reporting on risk on a regular basis to the Corporate Leadership Team (CLT), 
Cabinet, and Governance Standards and Audit Committee 

=== PDF PAGE 279 ===
276 
 
 
Risk Management Strategy 
The council is committed to delivering risk management to achieve its objectives, 
deliver effective and quality services, and safeguard the interests of the public. Risk 
management is integral to council planning, decision -making, and governance 
processes which apply to all departments, services areas and all staff. 
The process of identifying and evaluating risks is known as risk assessment.  By 
understanding the risks we face, we are better able to actively recognise where 
uncertainty surrounding events or outcomes exists and identify measures which can 
be taken to protect the council, its staff, residents, customers and assets from these 
risks. 
This strategy and policy provide a structured approach to identifying emerging risks 
as well as assessing and managing current risks.  It also incorporates a process for 
regularly reviewing and updating identified risks. 
This strategy will be reviewed on an annual basis and updated where required with 
regular reporting through governance and scrutiny committees. The council promotes 
continuous improvement by learning from near misses, incidents and best practice. 
Risk Appetite 
Risk Appetite is the amount of risk, at a broad level, that an organisation is willing to 
accept in pursuit of its strategic objectives. 
• Averse: The lowest level of risk appetite. At this level, an organisation is making risk 
avoidance one of its top priorities. ... 
• Minimalist: A low risk appetite. ... 
• Cautious: A moderate risk appetite. ... 
• Flexible: A high risk appetite. ... 
• Open: The highest level of risk appetite. 
The Council accepts that risk is an inherent part of innovation, pursuing new 
opportunities and delivering high quality services. It also accepts that it is not 
possible, practical, or desirable to eliminate all risk from its activities. 
The Council therefore seeks to manage all risk within acceptable levels (its ‘risk 
appetite’ or ‘tolerance’). While overall having a moderate tolerance of risk (all risk 
owners are expected to implement mitigation tactics for all amber/red risks/issues), 
the level of risk accepted will vary between the opportunities being sought, activities 
being delivered and the overall risk exposure from combined risk of a similar nature. 
The council recognises that its risk appetite to achieve the corporate priorities 
identified within its business plan could be described in general as an “informed and 
cautious” approach. Where significant risk arises, we will take effective control 
action to reduce these risks to an acceptable level. 

=== PDF PAGE 280 ===
277 
 
 
It is also recognised that a higher level of risk may need to be accepted, for example 
to support innovation in service delivery. To offset this there are areas where the 
council will maintain a very cautious approach for example in matters of compliance 
with the law, and public confidence in the council, supporting the overall “informed 
and cautious” position on risk. 
The primary function of “scoring” risks is to facilitate their prioritisation and 
assessment against risk appetite. This step involves determining the likelihood of 
the risk occurring and its impact should it occur. Please see the “Risk Scoring 
Section” below for further detail of the scoring mechanism and the definitions 
utilised at LCC to calculate the level of the risk: - Impact x Likelihood = Risk score. 
The table below confirms expectation of how scored risks should be managed: 
 
 
 
Scored Level of Risk Rating Expected Action 
 
 
High Risk 
 
 
15-24 
Risk to be monitored at least monthly, senior 
management would expect its proactive 
management incorporated into operational activity at 
maximise the chance of the risk moving down to 
Amber or Green 
 
Medium Risk 
 
5 -12 
Managers expected to undertake mitigating actions 
to minimise chance of risk increasing to Red and 
maximise the chance of reduction to Green. 
 
Low Risk 
 
1 - 4 
Risk scoring within tolerable level, risk to be 
monitored quarterly to minimise chance of risk 
increasing to Amber 
 
 
Risk Management Definitions 
 
b. Definitions 
A ‘Risk’ is defined as the “effect of uncertainty on objectives” (ISO 31000). Risk is the 
threat that an event or action may adversely affect an organisation’s ability to achieve 
its objectives and successfully execute its strategies. A risk can be a threat, obstacle, 
barrier, concern, problem or event that may prevent us fulfilling our objectives. 
‘Risk Management’ refers to a coordinated set of activities and methods that is used 
to direct an organisation and to control the range of risks that affect its ability to achieve 
objectives. 

=== PDF PAGE 281 ===
278 
 
 
A ‘Risk Management process’ is one that systemically applies management policies, 
procedures, and practices to a set of activities intended to establish the context, 
communicate and consult with stakeholders, and identify, analyse, evaluate, treat, 
monitor and review risk. 
An ‘Issue’ is an unexpected event happening in the present moment with a potential 
negative impact on achieving objectives. It is happening right now or just happened 
with immediate effect. 
Issues are management outside of the risk management process. Issues are current 
problems, questions, outstanding items, tasks or a request that exists in the 
immediate present. There is a strong element of fact surrounding it. An issue 
becomes a risk when the issue cannot be addressed and could continue or get worse. 
Definition of Risk Management 
Organisations exist to achieve their ambitions, aims and objectives. Risk 
Management is the process by which organisations methodically address and identify 
the risks that may prevent them from achieving these ambitions, aims and objectives. 
The intention is to achieve sustained benefit within each of their activities, and across 
the portfolio of all their activities. 
Ultimately, risk management is about creating a better understanding of the most 
important problems facing organisations. 
Risk is also implicit in the decisions all organisations take; how those decisions are 
taken will affect how successful they are in achieving their objectives. Decision 
making is, in turn, an integral part of the day -to-day existence and is particularly 
significant in times of change. Risk management therefore is a key component in the 
management of change and helps to support effective decision making. 
 
 
We endeavour to identify all risks facing the council and to monitor, manage and 
mitigate (where possible) all those risks which are deemed to be high (scored Amber 
or Red). Risks are monitored via Departmental Risk Registers, and key crosscutting 
risks to the council are also placed on the Key Strategic Risk Register (KSRR). 
c. Benefits of Risk Management 
 
In addition to the business and service benefits of our approach, we are required to 
undertake risk management because it forms part of the Annual Governance 
Statement. We must, therefore, demonstrate that we have a systematic strategy, 
framework and process for managing risk. 

=== PDF PAGE 282 ===
279 
 
 
However, the council recognises that the benefits of risk management far outweigh 
the requirement to undertake the activity and such benefits include: 
• Stronger ability to achieve our ambitions, aims and objectives as key risks are 
managed. 
• Better decision making as we are more aware of risk. 
• Ability to take advantage of opportunities because we understand the risks 
attached to them. 
• Better governance and the ability to demonstrate it to our stakeholders. 
• Reduction in failure, loss, damage and injury caused by risk 
• Improvement in our ability to adapt to change 
• Improvement in our corporate governance 
• Compliance with statutory and regulatory requirements 
• Supports a continuous improvement and learning culture. 
 
Types of Risks 
Strategic Risks 
These are the Council’s most serious risks and tend to be either broad in impact 
whereby they have the potential to impact significantly on the capability, vitality, or 
success of the Authority as a whole or they are deep in impact, that is the effects of 
the risk event occurring are especially serious. The approach to corporate risks sets 
the context for decisions at other levels in the council. The number of corporate risks 
will vary depending on the Council’s risk profile. 
However, generally corporate risks are the top ten to 15 most serious risks faced by 
the Council. A key strategic risk is likely to have one or more of the following 
characteristics: - 
• Strategic and cross-cutting, with the potential to impact on a range of different 
areas or functions; 
• Related to the Council’s ability to successfully deliver one or more corporate 
objectives; 
• Affects the outcomes sought from one of the Council's major programmes; 
• Operates over the medium or long-term; 
• The potential to impact negatively on the organisation’s capacity, for example by 
limiting, reducing or failing to maximise financial or human resources; 
• Linked to the organisation’s ability to successfully deliver transformational change 
and major initiatives, while continuing with business as usual; 
• Concerned with the wellbeing of residents, businesses and/or LBM staff; and may 
impact on the Council’s reputation 

=== PDF PAGE 283 ===
280 
 
 
Corporate risks are captured on the KSRR, which is owned by the Corporate 
Leadership Team (CLT). The KSRR is also presented to the Governance Standards 
and Audit Committee meetings each year as this committee is responsible for 
monitoring the effective development and operation of risk management. Risks from 
the Departmental Risk Registers can be escalated here as part of the ongoing review 
process. The KSRR is reviewed on a regular basis. For reporting purposes, the KSRR 
should always be updated with information that is suitable for public consumption. 
Key service risks should be recorded in the Departmental Risk Register. The risks 
should be owned by the relevant Head of Service (HoS) or Assistant Director and be 
reviewed on a regular, usually quarterly basis by Departmental Leadership Teams 
(DLTs). 
Operational Risks/ Service Delivery 
These are risks that relate to the day-to-day delivery of Council services and could 
relate to a wide variety of different types or sources of risk. These risks can include: 
• Systems failures 
• Inadequate resource management 
• Lack of skilled personnel 
• Supply chain interruptions 
• Cyber threats 
• Compliance breaches 
• Quality failures etc 
 
For the council, such risks might lead to delays, failures, reduced service quality, and 
increased costs, ultimately impacting the communities trust and satisfaction which 
could result in a reputational impact. 
 
Project/ Programme Risks 
These risks refer to potential challenges and issues that can affect the successful 
delivery of planned initiatives, infrastructure improvements, or community services. 
These types of risks may include: 
• Budget overruns 
• Delays due to unforeseen circumstances 
• Changes in regulatory requirements 
• Stakeholder misalignments 
• Resource shortages etc 
If not managed effectively, these types of risks could lead to incomplete projects, 
wasted public funds and reduced community benefit. 
Risk management is a key part of the ongoing management of projects and 
partnerships and is clearly defined in Merton's Approach to Projects (MAP). 

=== PDF PAGE 284 ===
281 
 
 
Partnerships Risks 
These risks involve the potential challenges and uncertainties that arise when working 
collaboratively with external organisations, businesses, and other government bodies. 
These risks may include: 
• Misaligned objectives 
• Unclear roles and responsibilities 
• Poor communication and breakdowns 
• Financial instability of partners 
• Differing governance standards etc 
Partnership risks could result in project delays, compromised service quality, or 
reputational harm. The council is involved in a wide range of partnerships to achieve 
our ambitions, aims and objectives.  It is vital we assess the risks to achievement 
within our key partnerships and ensure that they are monitored regularly. 
Our methodology for assessing and monitoring risks has been adopted by our key 
partnerships in order to ensure consistent scoring, and effective integration into our 
risk management system. 
 
Financial Risk Management 
Local government has faced unprecedented financial challenges in recent years that 
are likely to remain well into the next decade. The harsh financial economy faced by 
local authorities has led Central Government and the public sector accounting body 
CIPFA to start to consider how best to minimise the chance of further Section 114 
notices being released and providing early warnings of authorities being unable to 
balance their budgets. Within Merton the following activity is already undertaken: 
Budget Setting 
• Financial pressures caused by demographic pressures in Special Educational 
Needs, Placements and Adult Social Care have been monitored closely – ongoing 
demands have received some additional funding 
• Financial modelling within the Medium -Term Financial Strategy and Capital has 
been developed 
• Horizon spotting is used to improve response times to changes in Central 
Government funding. 
• Financial Risk thresholds are kept under review 
• Impact of Brexit and Covid 
 
Budget Monitoring: 
• All budgets are monitored monthly, and reviewed with outturn, current spend and 
commitments 
• Monthly review of progress on delivery of savings with management action 
• Monitoring resources are targeted at high-risk areas 

=== PDF PAGE 285 ===
282 
 
 
Year End Accounting 
• Reviewing closing issues to minimise the chance of the issues occurring again 
• A greater emphasis on quality control of working papers 
• More emphasis on reconciliation work within the financial year. 
• There is also a financial impact element to the authorities risk matrix which has 
been reviewed and simplified. Officers will continue to review activity and adopt 
best practice etc. where appropriate. 
 
Risk Management Approach 
In order to formalise and structure risk management, it is recognised there is an 
obvious and clear link with the business planning process and therefore risk 
management sits within the Financial Strategy, Systems, Regeneration and Risk 
Management team. 
 
Corporate Risks – corporate risks not contained on the KSRR are normally 
incorporated into either the Innovation and Change or Financial and Digital 
Directorates (previously Corporate Services) where and appropriate risk owner will 
be identified 
The overall council Budget Book, incorporating the individual service plans, sets out 
what a team, division, department, or the council as a whole, want to achieve within 
a specific time frame, as shown below. 
Figure 2 - Merton Performance Management Framework 
 
 
• CLT is ultimately accountable for delivering the council’s Budget Book therefore 
they are responsible for monitoring and reviewing the KSRR. 
• DLTs are responsible for their own services’ risk registers. 
• Divisions or teams are responsible for their own risk registers, if applicable. 

=== PDF PAGE 286 ===
283 
 
 
It is important that risks identified and assessed at an operational level can be 
escalated to a departmental or corporate level.  However, because a risk may have 
a great impact on a team it does not necessarily follow that it may have the same 
impact on the department, or the organisation as a whole. 
Ultimately, it is the respective management team which decides if a risk is an 
appropriate inclusion on its risk register. 
 
Risk Management Process 
The risk management process has 4 steps as outlined in figure 3, below. Underpinning 
each stage of the process is open communication and consultation with interested 
parties and stakeholders. This supports ensuring internal and external stakeholders 
are considered and involved in the council’s work to understand and define its risks. 
Figure 3 – Risk Management Process 
 
 
a. Risk Identification 
 
The identification of risks should be completed at all organisational levels and 
primarily, risks are identified in relation to the achievement of the council’s objectives. 
The objectives can be Strategic, Operational, Service or Project level. Risk 
identification exercises should be repeated periodically to ensure that new and 
emerging risks are identified and recorded on the risk register as appropriate. This 
support keeping an up-to-date risk profile. 
Risks can be identified in a number of forums, including: 
• Workshop sessions with management teams having open discussions (risk 
reviews, horizon scanning for risk identification, incident management) 

=== PDF PAGE 287 ===
284 
 
 
• Interviews (1:1 meetings) with responsible individuals (i.e. risk owners, key 
stakeholders) 
• Meetings with smaller groups of people (i.e. agenda team meetings, risk 
champions meetings) 
• Questionnaires 
Sources of information that could help inform risk identification include: 
• The Council’s Corporate Plan and Department or Service plans 
• Strengths, weaknesses, opportunities and threats (SWOT) analysis 
• PESTLE analysis (Political, Economic, Social, Technological, Legal, 
Environmental) 
• Historical incident and claims data 
• Audit reports (risks or issues raised by audit or any other internal/ external scrutiny 
body 
• Benchmarking and best practice comparisons 
• Process and control documentation 
• Performance reports 
• Financial reports (i.e. risks identified through budget setting process) 
• Internal or external research papers or statistical trends (i.e. supplier data, market 
research, insurance reports etc) 
• Health & safety/ business continuity risk assessments (e.g. scenario planning and 
simulation exercises) 
• Partnership, programme or project documentation (e.g. business case or project 
risk register) 
• Regulatory and legal requirements 
It is essential for risks to be defined and articulated properly at this stage to ensure the 
risk is properly understood by the risk owner and key stakeholders reading the 
recorded risk. This includes understanding the key causes and potential 
consequences of the risk which are separate fields on the risk register format. Not 
doing so can result in confusion about the exact nature of the risk, why it is a risk to 
the council and why it could occur. This could lead to inappropriate or ineffective risk 
controls being implemented, or the risk analysis being over or underestimated. 
Including key cases (real or perceived exposures/ causes) and potential 
consequences (worst likely scenario, significant impacts) can support identifying 
potential actions to treat the risk to reduce either the likelihood or impact. 

=== PDF PAGE 288 ===
285 
 
 
b. Risk Assessment 
 
Once a risk is identified, the risk matrix (see Figure 4, below) is the main tool for 
assessing each risk. As mentioned above, the analysis supports prioritising each risk, 
so we understand which risks are most significant and therefore are in need of greater 
attention, effort and resources. It also allows us to compare different types of risk with 
each other and gives a view of the council’s risk profile. 
Each risk should be assessed for the probability likelihood it will occur and the severity 
impacts if it did. The risk score is calculated by multiplying the impact score and the 
likelihood score. This assessment should be made at the inherent risk level (original 
level - no controls in place), the residual risk level (current – considering controls in 
place and working effectively) and at Target risk level (considered an acceptable level 
of risk). 
Probability assessment is applied relative to specific timeframes e.g. ‘operational’ risks 
will be assessed over a shorter timeframe than ‘strategic’ risks. 
Likelihood Criteria: 
• 6 – Very High 
• 5 – High 
• 4 – Significant 
• 3 – Possible 
• 2 – Low 
• 1 – Almost impossible 
The severity impact should be considered against the relevant objectives such as 
strategic risks should be scored against the council’s strategic objectives; department 
risks scored against department objectives; service risks scored against service 
objectives; project risks scored against the objectives of the project. The impact relates 
to the potential effects on an objective, activity, function or the whole organisation 
which may be negative. Risk assessment can be subjective to an individual therefore, 
using the definitions in the impact criteria (Appendix A) can support a more objectives 
assessment as the risk level are in the council’s context. 
Impact Measure: There are 4 impact descriptors as follows: 
• 4 – Very Serious 
• 3 – Serious 
• 2 – Moderate 
• 1 – Marginal 

=== PDF PAGE 289 ===
286 
 
 
Figure 4 – Risk Matrix 
 
Likelihood Impact 
6. Very high 4. Very Serious 
5. High 3. Serious 
4. Significant 2. Moderate 
3. Possible 1. Marginal 
2. Low 
1. Almost impossible 
Impact 
 
c. Risk Treatment 
 
The Risk Treatment step is ensuring appropriate and effective controls are in place 
and mitigating actions are decided for each risk. It is about turning the information 
gained about each risk from identification and analysis into appropriate action, to treat 
or not to treat the risk. The risk identification (causes and consequences) will support 
informing any specific actions that may be needed. 
There are four treatment options available if the judgement of the risk is further 
treatment is needed: 
• Treat: Implement procedures and controls to reduce the frequency or the severity; 
formulate a contingency plan to reduce service interruption 
 
• Tolerate: Bear losses out of normal operating costs following an informed decision 
to retain risk/monitor situation 
 
• Transfer: Transfer risk to another party, outsource and/or insurance 
 
• Terminate: Stop the activity or do it differently using alternative systems 
Risk Controls 
Most of the risks within the risk register will require some level of treatment to bring 
them within the acceptable appetite of the Council. When designing a control there are 
some key considerations your control must be; 
 
• Measurable and quantifiable, this will allow for you to check whether the control is 
performing as expected. 
• A consistent event, a control must happen on a regular basis to be effective, this could 
be hourly, daily, monthly or yearly. 
• Happening now, the control must be a current process that is taking place and is 
actively mitigating the risk 
Likelihood 
6 6 12 18 24 
5 5 10 15 20 
4 4 8 12 16 
3 3 6 9 12 
2 2 4 6 8 
1 1 2 3 4 
 1 2 3 4 
 

=== PDF PAGE 290 ===
287 
 
 
There are four main types of controls; these are described in the table below, you 
will often need a mix of these to effectively mitigate a risk, spread across different 
areas of the business. 
 
Control Type Description Examples 
 
Preventative 
 
Designed to stop errors and 
anomalies occurring 
• Adequate segregation of duties 
• Proper authorisation of 
transactions 
• Adequate documentation and 
control of asset 
 
 
 
Detective 
 
 
Aimed at finding problems before 
they become problems 
• Budget monitoring – to identify 
unexpected variances 
• Account reconciliation – to ensure 
ledger balances are accurate 
• Exception reports – to highlight 
unusual transactions or patterns 
• Bank reconciliations – to compare 
the ledger to bank statements 
 
 
 
Corrective 
 
These are implemented after 
problems have been identified by 
detective controls 
• Restoring data from backups after a 
system failure or data loss 
• Patching software vulnerabilities 
after a breach is detected 
• Retraining staff after errors or 
misconduct 
• Conducting post-incident reviews 
when something has gone wrong 
Directive Aim to ensure that risks are managed through formal direction • Codes of conduct • Professional codes of practice 
• Policies and procedures 
 
Specific actions agreed to further treat risks should be recorded on the relevant risk 
register with an appropriate timeframe agreed for implementing them. To support 
action tracking, a model such as ‘SMART’ – Specific, Measurable, Achievable, 
Realistic, Timely can support effective action implementation. It is essential that risk 
owners detail key actions which provide a measurable mitigation when reviewing 
their risks. 
d. Review & Monitor 
 
Regular and continuous review with scheduled risk reviews and ongoing monitoring of 
key indicators and warning signs ensure risks are continually considered and kept up-
to-date. 
Clear ownership and accountability are essential with designated individuals 
responsible for risk updates, reviews, and escalation/ de-escalation when necessary. 
Sharing risk information transparently across teams and stakeholders’ supports 
building trust and informs decision making. 

=== PDF PAGE 291 ===
288 
 
 
Each Operational area is responsible for updating and maintaining its own risk register. 
Monitoring and review of these risk registers should be done at least quarterly. 
Capturing lessons learnt from past events helps strengthen risk management 
processes and continuous improvement 
When any team reviews risk registers the following should be considered: 
• Have existing risks changed in any way? 
• Has the likelihood or impact score changed since the last review? 
• Have further actions, if they were needed, been implemented? 
• Are the controls in place to manage the risk effective and still working adequately? 
What assurance is available to support this judgement? 
• Are the controls in place appropriate to the level of risk? 
• Is the response overly cautious or is more attention needed? 
• Have all interested parties or stakeholders been consulted on the risk or 
communicated with in an appropriate way? 
• Are there any new or emerging risks not captured in the risk register? 
• Are there any risks that can be deleted or removed from the register? 
 
Recording Risks 
A Risk Register is the primary tool to capture and record the risks identified. It is a 
‘snapshot’ in time which reviewing and updating a risk register is essential to ensure 
the information is relevant and up to date. The council uses JCAD systems to record 
risks and issues. 
https://merton.jcadcore.com/secure/Dashboard/Dashboard 
 
Risk Management – New Risk Template is found on this page 
 
Governance/ Reporting Risks 
All risks on individual service risk registers are reviewed at Departmental Leadership 
Team (DLT) meetings with particular attention given to red or increasing amber risks. 
Risks are also checked for any cross -cutting implications. If the risk is high scoring 
and/or could have an impact across the organisation, then it must be included in Key 
Strategic Risk Register, which contains risks which could have a detrimental impact 
across the whole organisation should they occur. 
The Risk Management Strategy and KSRR are reviewed annually and approved by 
Council as a part of the budget process. There is an annual report to the Governance 
Standard and Audit Committee on progress and the sufficiency of Risk Management. 
See further roles and responsibilities are detailed in Appendix B 

=== PDF PAGE 292 ===
289 
 
 
Risk Management in Committee Reports 
When a report is submitted to a committee the author is required to complete a section 
on Risk Management. The committee should be informed of any significant risks 
involved in taking a recommended course of action, or if it decides not to follow the 
recommended course of action.  The risk assessment should follow the corporate risk 
management procedures and be scored using the risk matrix.  The report should also 
give details of any control measures (either proposed or existing) to manage any 
significant risks identified. Where appropriate, reference should be made to any existing 
risk(s). 
 
Report authors are advised to consult with the Financial Strategy, Systems, 
Regeneration and Risk Management team or their departmental Risk Champion, for 
further advice and to propose any risks to be considered for inclusion in the departmental 
or KSRR. 
 
Risk Management Practice during Extraordinary Events 
It is envisaged that the velocity of change during such an occurrence (e.g. Covid) will 
require the authority to maintain a separate risks and issues register to ensure an 
adaptable, flexible and frequent review to accurately track the movement of items. 
During these periods an additional risk log will be established, where by managers 
submit and update their risks directly via a secured, interactive shared portal (currently 
SharePoint). This information will then be reviewed and amended appropriately by 
senior management, DLTs, CLT and Members. 
 
Escalating/ De-escalating Risks 
A decision may also be taken at this time to escalate or de -escalate the risk, for 
example from a project manager to a sponsor or from a department to the executive. 
The following criteria may help this decision: 
To escalate: 
• Severity of impact increases becoming cross cutting with significant impacts 
across the council. 
• Likelihood probability rises due to changes in internal or external factors. 
• Audit report or checks show that controls in place are insufficient or failing with 
new vulnerabilities identified. 
• Legal implications where a risk could results in non-compliance, legal action or 
investigation. 
• Resources needed to manage the risks exceeds the current capacity and 
required more support from the council. 
• Stakeholder concern increases which requires senior management attention. 

=== PDF PAGE 293 ===
290 
 
 
To de-escalate: 
• Impact severity or likelihood probability reduces due to mitigation success or 
change in circumstances. 
• Controls in place are assessed as effective and working as intended to manage 
the risk. 
• The underlying causes or exposures have been address or no longer exists. 
• The risk can be managed at a lower level which is proportional to the risk. 
• The risk is no longer cross cutting but singular to a service or project. 
 
 
Monitoring Risks 
During the year, new risks will arise that have not previously been considered and 
there may be changes to existing risks. Therefore, the risk registers need to be 
regularly managed, with risk owners re -assessing their risks, re -scoring them if 
appropriate, and providing sufficient narrative in respect of the Control Measures they 
have in place (ie the actions which they are taking to mitigate against the risk). The 
reviews of risk registers should be managed by exception.  The reporting cycle as 
detailed below, takes place during April, July, October and January. 
 
 
All risks are reviewed according to the quarterly cycle shown in the table above, with 
a particular focus upon red risks, and also upon amber risks which have increased 
their risk score since the previous quarterly review. 
 
Removal of any risks from the registers must be approved by DLTs and CRMG prior 
to being presented to CLT. CRMG will only approve removal of a risk if it is scored 
green for a minimum of two consecutive reporting cycles (i.e., two quarters). There are 
otherwise no rigid guidelines for dropping risks from the registers because clear 
parameters are not always possible. A decision is sometimes taken to keep a low -
scoring risk in view on the basis that its status might change over a short period, or so 
those with an assurance role can be confident mitigation against a risk can be 
sustained. 

=== PDF PAGE 294 ===
291 
 
 
A flowchart showing how service, departmental, corporate and partnership risks are 
decided to be escalated and reported is shown in Appendix D. 
 
Training & Development 
The council is dedicated to ensuring all staff understand and have the knowledge to 
manage risks effectively through ongoing training and support as required. Training 
sessions will be delivered to focus on awareness and understanding, clarifying roles 
and responsibilities, and providing practical guidance on the risk management process 
and arrangements in this strategy and policy framework. 
By embedding risk management in everyday activities and decision -making, the 
council support staff managing risk proactively and continually improve the protection 
of services, resources, and communities.
